Engineering mechanics

Draw a free-body diagram and calculate stress and deformation, then determine whether yielding, instability or a growing crack limits a structure.

Each volume develops its arguments in sequence, with worked problems and links to experiments.

  • Stress, deformation and structural measurement

    Start from balance, compatibility and material response. Work through structural predictions with alternative load or support conditions and assess the measurements needed to test them.

Advanced study across the subject

Each sequence connects the course to detailed chapters in neighbouring subjects. The complete course PDF includes their derivations, examples and exercises.

  1. Elastic members assembled into mechanisms

    Choose a load path, propagate its compliance and determine where contact or a bonded connection changes the boundary conditions.

How the course fits together

  1. Chapters 1–4

    Draw free bodies and recover support reactions. A cut exposes axial forces, shear and bending moments. A material law and displacement compatibility turn those internal actions into extensions and beam deflections.

  2. Chapters 5–8

    Stress components combine on an inclined plane. Compressive load can also destabilise a whole member. Torsion and transverse shear explain why the location and direction of the largest stress depend on section geometry.

  3. Chapters 9–12

    Use compatibility to solve redundant supports and strain measurements to reconstruct the stress state. Plastic redistribution and crack growth introduce two different limits to the elastic load calculation.

Chapter 1 · Free bodies, moments and support reactions

Replace a distributed load by its force and moment, then determine which reactions a support can supply.

From first principles

Derive the force and moment conditions for a free body

Choose one body and mark the external loads and support reactions crossing its boundary. In an inertial frame, Newton's linear momentum balance for each mass element sums to a balance for the whole body. Internal action–reaction pairs cancel. Taking moments about a fixed point \(O\) similarly eliminates internal couples for ordinary continuum forces, leaving

\[\sum\mathbf F_{\rm ext}=\frac{d\mathbf P}{dt},\]
\[\sum\mathbf M_{O,\rm ext}=\frac{d\mathbf H_O}{dt}.\]

Static equilibrium requires both momenta to remain constant at zero. For planar loading these vector statements reduce to three scalar conditions. A distributed downward load \(q(x)\) contributes force and moment by summing its infinitesimal forces with their physical lever arms:

\[\sum F_x=0,\]
\[\sum F_y=0,\]
\[\sum M_O=0,\]
\[W=\int q(x)\,dx,\]
\[W\bar x=\int xq(x)\,dx .\]

The equations predict reactions only within the chosen support model. A roller can exert a normal force but no end moment. An upward-only contact cannot supply a negative normal reaction; such a mathematical solution calls for a different contact configuration.

Derivation and applications

Locate the resultant of a linearly varying distributed load

A beam support carries the effect of the complete loading, including where each small portion acts. Consider a rigid horizontal span of length \(L\) with a pin at its left end and a roller at the right. Let \(q_a\) and \(q_b\) be downward line loads at the ends, varying linearly in between. Use upward support forces \(R_A,R_B\). Ignore the beam weight unless already included in \(q\). The aim is to replace the distribution by a single force that reproduces both force and moment.

At position \(x\), a slice of length \(dx\) contributes \(q(x)dx\) downward. Its moment magnitude about the left end has the additional factor \(x\). Integrating first the force and then the moment gives

\[q(x)=q_a+(q_b-q_a)x/L,\]
\[W=\int_0^L q(x)dx=\tfrac12L(q_a+q_b).\]
\[M_A^{\rm load}=\int_0^L xq(x)dx=L^2\left[\frac{q_a}{2}+\frac{q_b-q_a}{3}\right]=\frac{L^2}{6}(q_a+2q_b).\]

If \(W\ne0\), its point of application is \(\bar x=M_A^{\rm load}/W\). Moment equilibrium about the pin and vertical force equilibrium now give the support readings:

\[R_B=\frac{L}{6}(q_a+2q_b),\]
\[R_A=\frac{L}{6}(2q_a+q_b).\]

For equal end loads the resultant is at midspan. For a triangle increasing from zero it acts at two thirds of the span from the zero end. These formulas predict external reactions; replacing the distribution by a single force would change the internal shear and moment between the supports.

Worked example

Predict the support readings of a loaded shelf

A \(2.40\) m shelf carries \(q_a=100\) N/m on the left increasing to \(q_b=400\) N/m on the right. The resultant is \(600\) N and its moment about the left end is \(864\) N m, placing it at \(1.44\) m. The right support reads \(360\) N and the left \(240\) N. Their sum and their moment reproduce both integrals independently.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Reconstruct a linear distribution from the scale readings

The same span now gives \(R_A=320\) N and \(R_B=280\) N. Adding the readings gives

\[q_a+q_b=2(600)/2.40=500\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

Subtraction gives

\[q_a-q_b=6(320-280)/2.40=100\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

Thus \(q_a=300\) and \(q_b=200\) N/m. If a point load could also be present, these two support readings alone would not uniquely determine the load shape.

OneSimulation laboratory · Distinguish force from deflection in a truss

Choose the body before writing equations

A platform scale under a shelf can read more than the weight placed directly above it. The measurement depends on all the other supports and the locations of all the applied forces. To predict it, imagine removing the shelf from its surroundings. Replace each lost contact by the force or couple that the surroundings could exert. Internal forces between parts of the isolated body cancel in pairs. The choice of boundary determines which forces are internal.

In a planar model a smooth roller on a horizontal track supplies one vertical reaction. A pin supplies two force components and admits rotation. A clamp supplies both force components and a reaction moment. A cable pulls along its length. A roller simply resting on a track can push, but a negative calculated upward reaction would require a hold-down or a change of contact. These restrictions are part of the physical model, even before a single number is inserted.

Sum forces and moments

A rigid body at rest in a plane obeys three independent scalar equilibrium equations. Choose an arbitrary point \(O\) as the moment centre. Taking upward force and counterclockwise moment as positive gives

\[\begin{aligned} \sum_i F_{x,i} &= 0,\\ \sum_i F_{y,i} &= 0,\\ \sum_i M_{O,i} &= 0. \end{aligned}\]

A force applied at \((x,y)\) relative to \(O\) contributes \(xF_y-yF_x\) to the last sum. Taking moments about a pin eliminates its two unknown forces at once. A specified couple has the same signed moment about every origin and contributes zero resultant force.

For a vertical load with downward intensity \(q(x)\) in N/m, a strip of width \(dx\) contributes a downward force of magnitude \(q(x)\,dx\). Integrate over the loaded span to obtain the force magnitude and its clockwise moment magnitude about the left end. An equivalent point load must reproduce both.

\[\begin{aligned} W&=\int q(x)\,dx,\\ W\bar x&=\int x\,q(x)\,dx,\\ \bar x&=\frac{\int x\,q(x)\,dx}{\int q(x)\,dx}. \end{aligned}\]

This replacement preserves the external reactions. When finding an internal force at a cut, integrate only the load actually lying on the chosen side.

Background · Why can the moment balance be taken about any point?

Keep all forces on the same free body, including support reactions. Let \(\mathbf a\) point from the first moment centre \(O\) to the second \(P\). Every force lever arm changes by the same vector. Applied couples have no lever arm and carry their original signs.

\[\mathbf M_P=\sum_i(\mathbf r_i-\mathbf a)\times\mathbf F_i+\sum_j\mathbf C_j=\mathbf M_O-\mathbf a\times\sum_i\mathbf F_i.\]

Once force balance holds, the two moment sums coincide. This makes a second choice of moment centre a diagnostic. A missing 100 N vertical load creates a 50 N m difference between sums taken about points 0.5 m apart horizontally. Repeating the moment sum about points displaced parallel to that missing force would hide the discrepancy.

Weigh a platform with a triangular load

A 4.0 m platform is pinned at its left end A and supported by a roller at B at its right end. Bulk material forms a triangular pile, loading the platform with intensity rising linearly from zero at A to 3.0 kN/m at B. A 2.0 kN crate sits 1.0 m from A. Platform self-weight has already been tared off. Write \(q(x)=q_Bx/L\). Integration gives

\[W=q_BL/2=6.0\,\mathrm{kN},\]
\[\bar x=(q_BL^2/3)/W=2L/3=2.667\,\mathrm{m}.\]

The resultant is closer to the heavily loaded end.

\[4B_y-6(2.667)-2(1)=0,\]
\[A_y+B_y=8.0\ {\rm kN}.\]

The right reaction is 4.50 kN and the left is 3.50 kN. There is no applied horizontal force, so \(A_x=0\). Check independently about B: the clockwise effect of the left reaction is \(3.50(4)=14.0\) kN·m and the two weights give the opposite moment \(6(1.333)+2(3)=14.0\) kN·m. Replacing the triangle by a uniform load of equal total weight would pass a force check and fail this moment check.

Predict the next scale reading

Move the crate 0.50 m to the right. Its force is unchanged, but its moment about A increases by 1.0 kN·m. The right reaction therefore rises by \(1.0/4.0=0.25\) kN and the left falls by the same amount. In practice this relation lets two load cells measure both weight and the position of a moving load. It assumes a stable support arrangement with known moment arms. Three vertical supports under a rigid platform introduce an extra unknown. Their individual readings then require information about deflections and support stiffness, which equilibrium alone cannot supply.

Find the load position from the reactions

An inverse problem uses the same equilibrium equations. A 2.0 m simply supported rail carries a single unknown load and sensors read 900 N at the left and 300 N at the right after taring the empty rail. The total applied downward load is 1200 N. Taking moments about the left support gives its position as \(300(2.0)/1200=0.50\,\mathrm m\). Repeating this reasoning for a distributed load gives the position of its resultant but cannot reconstruct its detailed distribution.

Determine when an assumed contact can exist

A support symbol on paper often hides a condition on the direction of its reaction. A dry foot resting on a horizontal floor can push on the floor, but it cannot pull the floor upwards. A stationary contact can transmit a range of tangential forces up to its friction limit. These statements convert an ordinary equilibrium problem into one with inequalities. First derive the forces required to maintain the proposed configuration. Then ask whether the contact can supply them. Assigning the maximum friction before writing equilibrium would generally give the wrong answer for a stationary body.

Consider a rectangular machine base supported on two narrow feet a distance \(b\) apart. Its centre of mass lies midway between them, its weight is \(W\), and a horizontal force \(P\) acts to the right at height \(h\). Write upward contact forces \(N_L,N_R\). Neglect the thickness of the contact patches and consider only motion in the vertical plane. Vertical equilibrium requires

\[N_L+N_R=W.\]

Taking moments about the midpoint between feet eliminates both unknown friction forces, which act along the floor. The right foot gains normal load as the left one loses it:

\[(N_R-N_L)b/2=Ph,\]
\[N_L=W/2-Ph/b,\]
\[N_R=W/2+Ph/b .\]

Both feet stay in contact only while \(P\le Wb/(2h)\). If both have the same coefficient \(\mu\), their combined available friction is

\[\mu(N_L+N_R)=\mu W.\]

Thus impending tip and impending slip are distinct limiting states. The precise sharing of tangential load between the feet depends on their compliance; these three rigid-body equations alone need not fix it.

Decide which motion starts first on a test stand

A movable test stand weighs 1800 N. Its feet are 0.80 m apart and the technician pushes horizontally on a handle 1.20 m above the floor. Suppose the measured static friction coefficient is 0.45. At a push of 400 N, the load shifts by \(400(1.20)/0.80=600\,\mathrm N\) to the right. The left reaction is 300 N and the right reaction is 1500 N. The available 810 N of total friction exceeds 400 N; a static configuration is possible. A calculation assigning each foot a friction force of 405 N would nevertheless violate the individual 135 N friction limit on the lightly loaded left foot.

Increase the push gradually. The tip threshold is \(1800(0.80)/[2(1.20)]=600\,\mathrm N\), below the 810 N sliding threshold. At 600 N the right foot alone carries 1800 N; the required friction ratio there is \(600/1800=0.333\), within the measured friction limit. Now relocate the handle to 0.60 m. The calculated tip threshold doubles to 1200 N, so the base begins sliding at 810 N while both feet still carry weight. The dividing height follows directly from equating both thresholds,

\[h=b/(2\mu)=0.889\,\mathrm m.\]

Lowering the handle changes the rotational demand without changing the total horizontal force that floor friction can transmit.

Locate a trolley from two support reactions

A simply supported 6 m runway weighs 12 kN uniformly and carries an 18 kN trolley. Take moments about the left support to find

\[R_B\,6=12(3)+18a.\]

Then close the vertical force balance. Suppose the right load cell reads 18 kN. Where is the trolley?

Chapter 2 · Axial stress, strain and compatibility

Determine how much a stepped member stretches and why a restrained temperature change produces force.

From first principles

Build the axial displacement from equilibrium, strain and a material law

Take a slender straight member loaded through its centroid, undergoing small axial deformation with no buckling or bending. Let \(N(x)>0\) denote tension, \(A(x)\) area and \(p(x)\) the axial force per unit length. Balance a slice between \(x\) and \(x+dx\): its two cut forces enter with opposite signs, while distributed load acts over the slice. Dividing by \(dx\) gives

\[N'(x)+p(x)=0,\]
\[\sigma_x(x)\simeq N(x)/A(x).\]

The stress relation assumes a nearly uniform section stress away from load introduction. The extension of the slice is \(u(x+dx)-u(x)\), so strain is \(du/dx\). For a linear elastic material with free thermal strain \(\alpha\Delta T\), Hooke’s law applies to strain remaining after this stress-free part is removed. Integrating along the actual member gives

\[u(b)-u(a)=\int_a^b\left[\frac{N(x)}{E(x)A(x)}+\alpha(x)\Delta T(x)\right]dx .\]

An imposed end displacement supplies a compatibility equation for unknown reaction forces. A freely heated unloaded bar extends with \(N=0\); setting total extension to zero generally creates thermal stress.

Background · Axial strain
\[\varepsilon=du/dx\]

This is engineering strain for small displacement gradients in a straight bar. A measured extension is an integral over the gauge length.

Derivation and applications

Separate assembly preload from compatible thermal strain

Two axial members attached in parallel to rigid end plates share one extension. Equal extensions need not mean equal elastic strains when their stress-free lengths change differently with temperature. Assume initially unstressed prismatic bars of common length \(L\), small deformation, uniform temperature increments, linear elastic moduli \(E_i\), areas \(A_i\) and free thermal strains \(\epsilon_i^T=\alpha_i\Delta T_i\). The plates remain parallel; an axial force resultant \(P\) acts through a guided fixture that prevents rotation.

Let \(\delta\) be the common end movement, positive for extension. Subtract thermal strain before applying Hooke’s law. The force in each bar then becomes

\[N_i=E_i A_i(\delta/L-\epsilon_i^T)=k_i(\delta-L\epsilon_i^T),\]
\[k_i=E_i A_i/L .\]

Equilibrium of the end plate sets the sum of the two member forces equal to \(P\). Solving before substituting back makes the compatible motion explicit:

\[\delta=\frac{P+k_1 L\epsilon_1^T+k_2 L\epsilon_2^T}{k_1+k_2}.\]

Separate the force in bar 1 into the usual load-sharing part and an internally balanced thermal part:

\[N_1=\frac{k_1P}{k_1+k_2}+\frac{k_1k_2}{k_1+k_2}L(\epsilon_2^T-\epsilon_1^T),\]
\[N_2=P-N_1.\]

The internal thermal forces cancel at the plates. They vanish under equal free thermal strains even when the elastic moduli differ. This model presumes the members can sustain any predicted compression without buckling or losing contact.

Worked example

Find stresses in a heated parallel link

A \(0.500\) m steel bar has \(E_1=200\) GPa, \(A_1=100\) mm² and \(\alpha_1=12\times10^{-6}\)/K. A second bar has \(E_2=100\) GPa, \(A_2=200\) mm² and \(\alpha_2=20\times10^{-6}\)/K. Both warm by \(50.0\) K with \(P=0\). Their axial stiffnesses both equal \(40.0\) MN/m. Free extensions would be \(0.300\) and \(0.500\) mm, so the shared extension is \(0.400\) mm. Bar 1 stretches elastically by \(0.100\) mm and carries \(4.00\) kN tension, or \(40.0\) MPa. Bar 2 carries \(4.00\) kN compression, or \(20.0\) MPa in magnitude.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Choose a load that relieves one member

After the same heating, pull the rigid plates apart with increasing external force. Half the load enters each bar, so \(P=8.00\) kN brings bar 2 from \(-4.00\) kN to zero. The extension then equals its own free extension of \(0.500\) mm. Bar 1 alone carries \(8.00\) kN, which follows independently from its \(0.200\) mm elastic extension.

OneSimulation laboratory · Distinguish force from deflection in a truss

Find the force carried through a section

Two shafts that support the same suspended mass can stretch by very different amounts. A load cell measures force, while a clearance specification usually limits displacement. Connect them by taking a cut normal to a straight member and replacing the removed material by its axial force \(N\). When the load is centred, changes slowly and is far from grips or abrupt changes in section, the nominal normal stress is

\[\sigma=N/A.\]

Adopt tension as positive. Near holes or grips the mean value remains useful for equilibrium, while local stresses can differ appreciably.

If a small material line of original length \(dx\) gains length \(du\), its engineering strain is \(du/dx\). Assume small strain, a uniform temperature across each section and a linear elastic material. Elastic strain is \(\sigma/E\), where Young’s modulus \(E\) has units of stress. Free thermal expansion adds the strain \(\alpha\Delta T\). Thus an element can change length even if its axial force is zero.

Reassemble the pieces

Multiply the strain by \(dx\) and add along the member. The displacement difference between its ends is

\[u(L)-u(0)=\int_0^L\frac{N(x)}{E(x)A(x)}\,dx+\int_0^L\alpha(x)\Delta T(x)\,dx.\]

Every term has units of length. Bars joined end to end carry the same force in a segment without an intervening axial load. Their extensions add, so their compliances \(L/(EA)\), measured in m/N, add. Bars between the same two rigid plates instead share the same extension. Their forces add, and their stiffnesses \(EA/L\), measured in N/m, add. Draw the connections before deciding which quantities are equal. The material with the larger modulus does not always carry the larger load because area and length also matter.

Background · What does a short extensometer actually measure?

A sensor spans two material marks with a finite separation \(L_g\). It reports their relative displacement divided by the original separation. When stiffness varies within the gauge length, this reading weights the local strains by their lengths.

\[\bar\epsilon_g=\frac{u(x+L_g)-u(x)}{L_g}=\frac{1}{L_g}\int_x^{x+L_g}\frac{N(s)}{E(s)A(s)}\,ds.\]

Take a 20 mm gauge spanning equal lengths of two segments with areas \(A\) and \(2A\), carrying the same tensile force in the same material. The indicated strain is \(0.75N/(EA)\). A gauge located entirely on the thin segment reads \(N/(EA)\). Moving the marks changes the inferred modulus if the analysis treats either reading as a point value at the step.

Stretch a two-material tie

A steel section is 0.60 m long with area 120 mm² and \(E_s=200\) GPa. An aluminium section continues another 0.40 m, has area 200 mm² and \(E_a=70\) GPa. Pull on the joined tie with 12 kN at constant temperature. Equilibrium gives 12 kN in both pieces. The nominal stresses are 100 MPa and 60 MPa respectively. Their elongations are

\[12\,000(0.60)/(200\times10^9\,120\times10^{-6})=0.300\,\mathrm{mm},\]
\[12\,000(0.40)/(70\times10^9\,200\times10^{-6})=0.343\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The end displacement is 0.643 mm. The aluminium extends more although its nominal stress is smaller, because its compliance is larger.

Dividing total force by total displacement gives a tie stiffness of 18.7 MN/m. This is smaller than the stiffness of either isolated piece. Checking this limit catches an accidental parallel-spring calculation. The stress calculation still treats each piece separately. Averaging their areas first would conceal the location of the larger nominal stress.

Install the tie between rigid walls

Now let the same tie fit exactly between rigid walls with zero force at its installation temperature. Raise the uniform temperature by 30 K. Take \(\alpha_s=12\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}\) and \(\alpha_a=23\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}\). If free, the pieces would extend by 0.216 and 0.276 mm. The walls prescribe zero total displacement, so an elastic shortening of 0.492 mm must develop. Multiplying this mismatch by the combined stiffness gives \(N=-9.18\) kN. The negative sign denotes compression.

A force balance on the entire tie merely says the two wall reactions are opposite. Their magnitude came from the displacement condition. Real supports deflect, giving part of the expansion back to the tie and reducing the force. If the tie is long and slender, check whether it can remain straight under this force before interpreting the compressive stress.

Test the difference between member force and extension

In a statically determinate pin-jointed truss the axial forces follow from joint equilibrium before the bar areas enter the displacement calculation. Predict both outputs when every bar area is doubled at unchanged geometry and load. If the compressive top chord initially carries 75 kN and the monitored joint moves down 3.219 mm, which reading changes, and by what factor? Check the two runs and explain the final rounding using \(\delta_i=N_iL_i/(E_iA_i)\). Open the truss experiment.

Integrate strain in a tapered member

A weight-saving tie may taper along its length although equilibrium still gives the same force at every cross-section. Its compliance cannot in general be calculated using the arithmetic mean area. Let a straight elastic bar of length \(L\) have uniform thickness and linearly varying width, so its area changes linearly from \(A_0\) to \(A_1\). Apply an axial end force \(P\), neglect distributed body force, take constant Young’s modulus and exclude local stress disturbances near load introduction. Isolating any segment gives \(N=P\); compatibility between adjacent slices gives

\[du/dx=P/[EA(x)].\]

Set \(A(x)=A_0+(A_1-A_0)x/L\). Changing integration variable from position to area produces a logarithm:

\[\delta=\int_0^L\frac{P}{EA(x)}\,dx=\frac{PL}{E(A_1-A_0)}\ln\!\frac{A_1}{A_0}.\]

This expression is unchanged if the member is turned end for end. Its limit as \(A_1\) approaches \(A_0\) follows from \(\ln(1+z)/z\to1\), recovering \(PL/(EA_0)\). The effective uniform area for extension is therefore a logarithmic mean. Narrow regions contribute disproportionately to compliance because strain depends on the reciprocal of area. The stress maximum still occurs at the smallest cross-section; a stiffness-equivalent uniform area does not reproduce it.

Size a suspended gauge bar from two different requirements

Take an aluminium tie with \(L=1.20\,\mathrm m\), \(A_0=120\,\mathrm{mm^2}\), \(A_1=360\,\mathrm{mm^2}\), \(E=70\,\mathrm{GPa}\), and \(P=6.0\,\mathrm{kN}\). Using newtons and millimetres, its elongation is

\[6000(1200)\ln3/[70000(240)]=0.471\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The arithmetic mean area 240 mm² would predict 0.429 mm, about 9% too low. The actual peak nominal stress is \(6000/120=50.0\,\mathrm{MPa}\), and the far larger end sees 16.7 MPa. A strain gauge near the large end therefore underestimates the average elongation if its measured strain is simply multiplied by the whole length.

A different loading mechanism matters for a very long uniform member. Suspend a uniform steel gauge wire of length \(L\) from its top with no end load, and measure \(x\) upwards from its free bottom. The force at \(x\) carries the weight below, \(N(x)=\rho A g x\). The area cancels from the strain \(N/(EA)=\rho gx/E\), so its total extension is \(\rho gL^2/(2E)\). Taking \(L=30\,\mathrm m,\ \rho=7850\,\mathrm{kg/m^3}\) and \(E=200\,\mathrm{GPa}\) gives 0.173 mm. Doubling the wire diameter halves neither the strain nor the elongation under self-weight, since the added area also adds weight. Enlarging the wire does reduce the separate extension caused by a fixed instrument hung from its end.

A practical way to separate the wire’s two contributions is to measure extension with two different end weights while leaving the wire itself in place. The difference between readings is \(\Delta P L/(EA)\), since self-weight and any fixed initial offset are the same in both tests. A plot of extension against end load therefore has slope \(L/(EA)\) and a nonzero intercept if its zero refers to the unstressed wire. If an instrument was instead zeroed after the wire was suspended, the intercept disappears from the record. Stating the reference condition prevents it from being misinterpreted as evidence for or against self-weight deformation.

Creep and relaxation put different constraints on the same bar

A polymer strip may continue to extend after a testing machine reaches its target force. In another test the machine holds the extension fixed and the force decreases. In each case the energy-storing part of the material can exchange load with a time-dependent internal mechanism. Represent the simplest such mechanism by a spring of modulus \(E_1\) in series with a viscous dashpot of viscosity \(\eta\). This Maxwell branch carries common stress \(s\) and its strains add:

\[\dot\varepsilon=\dot s/E_1+s/\eta.\]

To represent a solid with finite long-time stiffness, put a second spring \(E_\infty\) in parallel. The specimen stress is

\[\sigma=E_\infty\varepsilon+s.\]

Assume small uniaxial strain, uniform temperature and an initially relaxed specimen.

At prescribed step strain \(\varepsilon_0\), the dashpot cannot change length instantaneously, so both springs initially contribute. Holding the strain sets \(\dot s=-sE_1/\eta\). Hence \(\sigma(t)=\varepsilon_0(E_\infty+E_1 e^{-t/\tau_r})\) with relaxation time \(\tau_r=\eta/E_1\). Under a maintained stress \(\sigma_0\), substitute \(s=\sigma_0-E_\infty\varepsilon\) into the branch equation. The result is

\[\tau_c\dot\varepsilon+\varepsilon=\sigma_0/E_\infty,\]

with \(\tau_c=\eta(E_\infty+E_1)/(E_\infty E_1)\). The initial strain is \(\sigma_0/(E_\infty+E_1)\) and the creep strain approaches \(\sigma_0/E_\infty\). The two time constants differ even for the same specimen.

A 100 mm strip of area 20 mm² has \(E_\infty=100\) MPa, \(E_1=300\) MPa and \(\eta=6000\) MPa s. Hold its extension at 0.50 mm. The initial stress is 2.00 MPa, corresponding to 40 N. After 20 s it is \(0.005[100+300/e]=1.052\) MPa or 21.0 N. The long-time force is 10 N. Instead hold a previously relaxed strip at 20 N. Its stress stays at 1.00 MPa, giving initial extension 0.25 mm and final extension 1.00 mm. The creep time is 80 s. After that time extension is

\[1.00-(1.00-0.25)/e=0.724\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Matching one force reading at one time cannot establish which boundary condition was imposed. A machine compliance comparable to the specimen’s compliance moves the test between these ideal limits.

Practise on sheet 1 · Infer a relaxation model from a displacement-hold test. Further reading · MIT · 3.11 Mechanics of Materials · Engineering Viscoelasticity, §4.1 The Maxwell Spring-Dashpot Model and §4.2 The Standard Linear Solid (Maxwell Form).

Can a compliant support relieve thermal stress?

Heat an elastic bar by a uniform temperature increment. One end is fixed, the other pushes a linear spring. Compatibility gives

\[\alpha L\Delta T=F L/(EA)+F/k_s.\]

Normalise the displacement by free expansion and the force by the fully restrained value.

Problem sheet 1 · Equilibrium and axial compatibility

Draw each isolated body, state the sign convention and identify the compatibility condition before substituting numbers.

1. Find a load from two readings

A 5 m platform with a pin at the left end and a roller at the right weighs 6 kN uniformly. An added crate makes the reactions 4.2 kN and 5.8 kN respectively. (a) Determine the crate weight. (b) Find its position measured from the left. (c) Slide it 0.50 m left. Predict both new readings and show how their sum and difference respond.

Work the two balances

The total vertical load is \(4.2+5.8=10.0\) kN, so the crate weighs 4.0 kN. Taking moments about the left support gives

\[5.8(5)=6(2.5)+4x;\]

hence \(x=3.50\) m. Moving left removes \(4(0.50)=2.0\) kN·m from the clockwise load moment. The right reading falls by \(2.0/5=0.40\) kN to 5.4 kN, while the left rises to 4.6 kN. Total weight remains 10 kN. The right-minus-left difference decreases from 1.6 to 0.8 kN because one reading rises while the other falls.

2. Let two bars share one load

Rigid parallel plates are joined by a 0.40 m long steel bar (\(E=200\) GPa, \(A=100\) mm²) and an equal-length aluminium bar (\(E=70\) GPa, \(A=200\) mm²). The plates are guided to remain parallel. A centred 17 kN tensile load separates them. (a) Find each stiffness. (b) Determine the extension and forces. (c) Compare the two strains and two stresses.

Work the common-displacement condition

The stiffnesses \(EA/L\) are 50 MN/m and 35 MN/m. The common extension is

\[17000/(85\times10^6)=0.200\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Steel takes 10.0 kN and aluminium 7.0 kN, summing to 17 kN. Both strains are \(0.0002/0.40=0.00050\), but the stresses are 100 and 35 MPa. Equal length and equal displacement enforce equal strain. Different moduli turn equal strain into different stress, and different areas convert those stresses to forces.

3. Close a gap before loading a support

A stress-free 1.20 m steel bar points toward a rigid stop with a 0.18 mm gap. Its other end is fixed. Use \(A=150\) mm², \(E=200\) GPa and \(\alpha=12\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}\). (a) How much warming closes the gap? (b) Find the compressive force and stress after a uniform 30 K rise. (c) Interpret a calculation that uses all 30 K as a restrained rise.

Separate free and restrained stages

Free expansion closes the gap at \(0.00018/(1.20\alpha)=12.5\) K. The subsequent 17.5 K would add another 0.252 mm of expansion, which the stop prevents. Compatibility now requires elastic strain \(-\alpha(17.5)=-0.000210\). The resulting stress is −42.0 MPa and force −6.30 kN. Treating all 30 K as restrained would give −72 MPa and would incorrectly predict a reaction before contact. This piecewise solution assumes the bar remains straight and the stop carries compression.

Infer a relaxation model from a displacement-hold test

A 40 mm² specimen held at fixed strain 0.004 requires 48 N immediately, 24 N after a long hold and 32.83 N after 30 s. Infer both spring moduli and the branch viscosity. Predict the long-time strain when a fresh specimen instead carries 32 N.

Show the worked solution

The instantaneous and long-time stresses are 1.20 and 0.60 MPa. Dividing by strain gives \(E_\infty+E_1=300\) MPa and \(E_\infty=150\) MPa, hence \(E_1=150\) MPa. The excess force fraction after 30 s is \((32.83-24)/(48-24)=0.368\), so \(\tau_r\simeq30\) s and \(\eta=4500\) MPa s. At 32 N the maintained stress is 0.80 MPa. The strain starts at \(0.8/300=0.00267\) and tends to \(0.8/150=0.00533\). Its creep time is 60 s, so transferring the relaxation time directly to the force-hold test would predict the wrong rate.

Revisit chapter 2 · Creep and relaxation put different constraints on the same bar.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Free bodies, moments and support reactions; Axial stress, strain and compatibility. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Infer a truss area without mistaking it for a height change

On the 2 m high reference truss a 100 kN load produces 3.219 mm of downward displacement and a top-chord force of −75.0 kN. A second build gives 2.146 mm and the same chord force. Estimate the uniform bar area required if the geometry and modulus stayed fixed and the baseline area was 1000 mm². Test the alternative explanation that the height alone increased to 3 m.

Work through the solution

With unchanged geometry an internally determinate pin-jointed truss has member forces fixed by equilibrium. Each member’s elastic extension is \(NL/(EA)\), so uniform area scaling divides the complete displacement by the same factor. Thus \(A_2=1000(3.219/2.146)=1500\) mm². Raising height to 3 m gives −50.0 kN and 2.659 mm in the laboratory. The measured force already rules out that specific alternative. A uniform change in modulus remains indistinguishable from the inferred area change using these two readings alone.

Chapter 3 · Shear force and bending moment

Construct the internal-force diagrams of a simply supported beam and locate the section with the largest nominal bending stress.

From first principles

Obtain shear and moment from the equilibrium of a beam slice

Let a planar beam carry a downward line load \(q(x)\). Use positive sagging moment \(M\) and the shear sign convention that a positive shear raises \(M\) with increasing \(x\). Isolate the material between two closely spaced vertical cuts. The shear on the right face differs from the left by its spatial change, while the distributed load has resultant \(q(x)dx\) to first order. Vertical force balance gives

\[V(x+dx)-V(x)=-q(x)\,dx,\]
\[dV/dx=-q(x).\]

Moment balance about the left cut includes the shear acting at a lever arm \(dx\). The distributed load on this short interval produces only a term proportional to \(dx^2\). Dividing by \(dx\) and taking the limit leaves

\[dM/dx=V,\]
\[M(b)-M(a)=\int_a^b V(x)\,dx.\]

Support reactions set integration constants. A downward concentrated force creates a downward jump in shear, found by integrating across its position; an applied couple causes a jump in moment. Between concentrated actions, a zero of shear identifies a stationary value of moment, which may locate the most highly stressed fibre.

Derivation and applications

Construct a bending-moment diagram directly from load integrals

The maximum bending stress depends on moment, not necessarily on where the distributed loading is largest. Consider a simply supported span \(L\) carrying a triangular line load \(q(x)=q_0x/L\) directed down. Define positive sagging moment \(M\) and positive shear \(V=dM/dx\). The pin and roller transmit no end couples. Find reactions before cutting the span: the resultant load \(q_0L/2\) acts at \(2L/3\) from the left. Its moment balance therefore gives

\[R_A=q_0L/6,\]
\[R_B=q_0L/3,\]
\[R_A+R_B=q_0L/2.\]

A cut at \(x\) isolates the left support and the load between \(0\) and \(x\). Vertical equilibrium gives the support reaction minus the integral of all line load up to that cut:

\[V(x)=R_A-\int_0^x q(s)\,ds=\frac{q_0L}{6}-\frac{q_0x^2}{2L}.\]

Every small line force contributes a moment arm \(x-s\). Equivalently integrate \(V\) and fix the integration constant by \(M(0)=0\). Both routes give

\[M(x)=R_Ax-\int_0^x(x-s)q(s)\,ds=\frac{q_0Lx}{6}-\frac{q_0x^3}{6L}.\]

At the far support \(M(L)=0\), a check on the moment boundary condition rather than an additional reaction. Since the shear decreases monotonically across the span, its unique zero locates the maximum bending moment. A point couple, if present, would require a local jump in moment and could invalidate this smooth-maximum argument.

Worked example

Find the location of the largest moment

On a \(3.00\) m span let \(q_0=6.00\) kN/m. The end reactions are \(3.00\) and \(6.00\) kN. Setting \(V=0\) gives

\[x=L/\sqrt3=1.732\,\mathrm{m}.\]

Substitution yields \(M_{\max}=q_0 L^2/(9\sqrt3)=3.46\) kN m. The location lies left of the resultant at \(2.00\) m. Replacing the triangle by a single force there would give correct reactions but an incorrect peak moment.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Reconstruct a distributed load from section readings

A second unloaded-at-the-left triangular span has \(L=2.00\) m and a measured midpoint moment \(M(L/2)=0.750\) kN m. Substituting \(x=L/2\) into the exact diagram gives

\[M=q_0 L^2/16.\]

Thus \(q_0=3.00\) kN/m. The midpoint shear should be \(q_0 L/24=0.250\) kN. Moment and shear measured independently test whether the assumed linear load profile is plausible.

Read a section cut as an equilibrium problem

A transfer rail carries a trolley and the distributed weight of its own structure. Its supports may be strong enough while the rail still bends excessively at an interior section. Start with reactions for the entire beam. Next isolate the part from its left end to a cut at coordinate \(x\). The portion discarded from the drawing exerts an axial force, a shear force and a bending moment on the cut. For a horizontal beam with vertical loading the axial force is zero.

Use downward distributed load \(q(x)>0\), take \(M>0\) for sagging and define positive shear \(V\) by the resultant upward external load on the left-hand part. Thus a positive \(V\) corresponds to a downward internal shear on its right cut face. Choose the signs once and test a cut just to the right of the left support. With a positive left reaction the first value of the shear diagram must be positive.

Build a diagram from the loading

Take an element between \(x\) and \(x+dx\). Its external load increases the downward resultant by \(q\,dx\), so \(V(x+dx)-V(x)=-q\,dx\). Moment balance retains the term \(V\,dx\); the distributed load times its half-element arm is second order in \(dx\). Passing to the limit gives

\[\frac{dV}{dx}=-q(x),\]
\[\frac{dM}{dx}=V(x).\]

A point load produces a finite jump in \(V\) and a change of slope in \(M\). The bending moment is continuous there unless an applied concentrated couple acts. On an unloaded interval shear is constant and moment is a straight line. Under uniform loading the moment is parabolic. At a simple end support without an applied end couple the bending moment is zero. These four facts are often enough to find a sign mistake before calculating stress.

Combine a point load and distributed weight

Let a simply supported 6.0 m rail carry uniform weight 2.0 kN/m over its full span and a 6.0 kN trolley 2.0 m from the left end. The total downward load is 18 kN. Moment balance about the left gives \(6R_B=12(3)+6(2)=48\) kN·m, so \(R_B=8\) kN and \(R_A=10\) kN. To the left of the trolley, \(V=10-2x\) kN and \(M=10x-x^2\) kN·m for \(x\) in metres. At \(x=2\), \(M=16\) kN·m. Shear falls from 6 kN to zero on crossing the trolley.

To the right of the trolley, \(V=4-2x\) kN and \(M=10x-x^2-6(x-2)\) kN·m. Shear is negative throughout \(2<x<6\); the moment therefore falls to zero at the right support. The largest bending moment is 16 kN·m under the trolley. At the right end \(V(6^-)=-8\) kN. The upward reaction returns it to zero, another check on the construction.

Translate the moment into a fibre stress

For a symmetric homogeneous section undergoing small elastic curvature, strain varies linearly through depth. Integrating the moments of the Hookean normal stresses about the neutral axis gives

\[M=EI\kappa.\]

A fibre a distance \(c\) from that axis has stress magnitude \(Mc/I=M/Z\), where \(Z=I/c\) is the section modulus. Take a rectangular section 80 mm wide and 160 mm deep. Then \(Z=bh^2/6=3.41\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^3}\), giving a nominal bending stress of 46.9 MPa under the trolley. At a positive sagging moment the bottom is in tension. A local wheel-contact stress or weld stress requires its own length scale and model.

What jumps under an applied couple?

A concentrated transverse force produces a jump in shear. A concentrated moment produces a jump in the bending-moment diagram. For example, apply only a clockwise couple of 4 kN m midway along a 2 m simply supported beam. Global moment equilibrium gives a right reaction of 2 kN upwards and a left reaction of 2 kN downwards. The shear stays at −2 kN between supports. The moment thus falls from zero to −2 kN m on the left half, jumps to +2 kN m at the couple and falls to zero at the right support. The two zero end moments provide a check on the jump direction.

Derive an influence line at a fixed section

A bending-moment diagram follows a fixed loading pattern along a beam. A travelling wheel poses the converse question: how does the moment at one strain gauge vary as the load crosses the span? Take a simply supported beam of span \(L\) and a downward point load \(P\) located at \(z\) from the left support. Equilibrium gives

\[R_A=P(L-z)/L.\]

Keep the section coordinate \(a\) fixed while changing \(z\). The moment at \(a\) must be evaluated from a cut that contains the wheel only if the wheel lies to its left.

When \(z<a\), the moment is \(R_Aa-P(a-z)\); when \(z>a\), it is \(R_Aa\). Divide through by \(P\) to obtain an influence ordinate \(i_M\), whose units are length. The two linear expressions agree when the wheel crosses the gauge:

\[i_M(z)=\begin{cases}z(L-a)/L,&0\le z\le a,\\ a(L-z)/L,&a\le z\le L.\end{cases}\]

The ordinate is zero when the force is directly over a support and reaches \(a(L-a)/L\) at the gauge. For a distributed load \(w(z)\) the moment is the integral of \(w i_M\) because a short loaded length contributes \(w(z)\,dz\) of force. This is an application of superposition in a beam with fixed geometry and fixed support conditions. A support that lifts off would invalidate the same influence line.

Place a pair of unequal wheels to maximise one reading

Consider a 6 m span with a gauge at \(a=2.0\,\mathrm m\). A carriage has a front wheel carrying 8 kN and a rear wheel carrying 4 kN, a fixed 1.5 m behind it. It travels towards increasing \(z\). To maximise the moment, move both loads along the triangular influence line. The weighted sum is piecewise linear in carriage position, so its maximum occurs at a change of slope or over a level interval. The changes happen when a wheel enters the span, passes the gauge or exits. There is no need to sample many intermediate positions.

With the front wheel over the gauge the rear wheel is at 0.50 m. The ordinates are \(2(4)/6=1.333\) m and \(0.5(4)/6=0.333\) m, giving \(M=8(1.333)+4(0.333)=12.0\,\mathrm{kN\,m}\). With the rear over the gauge the front is at 3.5 m. Its ordinate is \(2(6-3.5)/6=0.833\) m, and the moment is \(4(1.333)+8(0.833)=12.0\,\mathrm{kN\,m}\) again. Between these positions the front contribution falls at \(8(2/6)\) kN for each metre moved, exactly balancing the rear contribution rising at \(4(4/6)\) kN per metre. The peak is a plateau, so several carriage positions give exactly the same design moment at this gauge.

For a narrow strain gauge on the bottom fibre of a symmetric elastic cross-section, the measured strain follows this moment history through \(\varepsilon=M y/(EI)\). Once the two wheels straddle the gauge, a flat measured strain interval is therefore physically plausible even though both wheels continue moving. A gauge near another section has a different influence line and need not show the same plateau.

Find curvature and stress in a bonded two-material beam

A homogeneous beam has its elastic neutral axis at the geometric centroid. A timber strip bonded to an aluminium cap can carry much larger normal stress on one side of their interface even though both materials have identical longitudinal strain there. Assume the interface prevents slip, each material is linear elastic, the beam is slender and no net axial force acts. With depth \(y\) measured from a common datum, compatible strain is \(\epsilon_x=-\kappa(y-y_n)\) and stress in layer \(i\) is \(E_i\epsilon_x\). The unknown neutral height \(y_n\) follows from axial force equilibrium:

\[\sum_i E_i A_i(\bar y_i-y_n)=0,\]
\[y_n=\frac{\sum_i E_iA_i\bar y_i}{\sum_i E_iA_i}.\]

Now sum moments of the layer stresses about this axis. The effective bending rigidity is \(D=\sum_i E_i[I_{i,c}+A_i(\bar y_i-y_n)^2]\), giving curvature magnitude \(M/D\). Dividing all moduli by one reference modulus gives the equivalent transformed-section construction. Taking an arithmetic mean modulus and the geometric centroid separately generally gives the wrong neutral position as well as the wrong rigidity.

As an independent worked section, bond two strips each 20 mm wide and 5 mm thick. Let the lower strip have \(E=70\) GPa and the upper strip \(E=210\) GPa. Measured from the bottom, their centroids are at 2.5 and 7.5 mm and each area is 100 mm². Thus

\[y_n=(2.5+3\times7.5)/4=6.25\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Each layer has \(I_c=208.33\,\mathrm{mm^4}\). Their distances from the neutral axis are 3.75 and 1.25 mm. The resulting rigidity is \(1.896\times10^8\,\mathrm{N\,mm^2}\). A sagging 20 N m moment gives

\[\kappa=1.055\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{mm^{-1}}.\]

The lower surface carries 46.2 MPa tension and the upper carries 83.1 MPa compression. At the interface \(y=5\) mm the strain is continuous at about \(132\) microstrain in tension, but the two normal stresses are 9.23 and 27.7 MPa. Such a longitudinal stress jump is compatible with a sound bond. Delamination would instead invalidate the assumed strain compatibility.

A channel can twist even when a force passes through its centroid. Chapter 4 · Elastic curves and virtual work

Predict a specified displacement from the bending-moment field and distinguish a stiffness limit from a stress limit.

From first principles

Why cantilever stiffness scales as the inverse cube of span

Take a straight slender beam clamped horizontally, with a downward end force \(P\). The empirical inputs are small-strain linear elasticity and the beam assumption that plane sections remain normal to the deflected centreline. Denote its centroidal bending rigidity by \(EI\). With constant section and modulus the stress moment of the stretched and compressed fibres is proportional to curvature. With downward deflection positive, equilibrium of a cut at \(x\) from the clamp supplies

\[M(x)=P(L-x),\]
\[w''(x)=M(x)/(EI).\]

Curvature is the rate at which slope changes. Integrating it once gives the slope, and integrating slope gives deflection. Combine both integrations into a weighted curvature integral: curvature occurring near the clamp rotates a longer remaining segment than curvature near the tip. The clamp fixes initial slope and displacement to zero, hence

\[w(L)=\int_0^L(L-x)w''(x)\,dx=\frac{P}{EI}\int_0^L(L-x)^2dx=\frac{PL^3}{3EI}.\]

This is a displacement prediction under small slopes, not just a stress limit. Its input \(M=EIw''\) is established from individual fibre strains and the neutral-axis condition in the detailed derivation. Short beams can also require shear deflection.

Background · Second moment of area
\[I=\int_A y^2\,dA\]

Measure the transverse coordinate from the stated bending axis. This section property has units of length to the fourth power and enters the flexural rigidity.

Derivation and applications

From the extension of a fibre to cantilever curvature and displacement

Why does section depth enter a bending calculation through a second moment of area? Take a straight prismatic cantilever with small slopes, uniform Young’s modulus \(E\), linear elastic response and slender dimensions. Euler–Bernoulli kinematics assumes that cross-sections remain plane and normal to the deflected centreline. Measure \(x\) from the clamp and transverse deflection \(w\) downward. Measure cross-section coordinate \(y\) downward from an initially arbitrary reference line. Sections separated by \(dx\) rotate relative to one another by \(d\theta\); a fibre at \(y\) shortens relative to the reference line by \(y\,d\theta\).

\[\epsilon_x(y)=\epsilon_0-y\,d\theta/dx\simeq\epsilon_0-yw''(x),\]
\[\sigma_x=E\epsilon_x .\]

With no imposed axial resultant, the integral of this normal stress must vanish. Writing \(\bar y=A^{-1}\int_A y\,dA\), we obtain the strain of the reference line, and thereby identify the zero-strain fibre:

\[0=N=\int_A E(\epsilon_0-yw'')\,dA,\]
\[\epsilon_0=\bar y w'',\]
\[\sigma_x=-E(y-\bar y)w''.\]

Define bending moment positive for this downward curvature. Each fibre contributes its force times its lever arm about the neutral axis. Integrating introduces the centroidal second moment \(I\):

\[M=-\int_A(y-\bar y)\sigma_x\,dA=EIw'',\]
\[I=\int_A(y-\bar y)^2\,dA.\]

A downward tip load \(P\) gives \(M(x)=P(L-x)\) by equilibrium of the portion beyond the cut. For constant \(EI\), integrate twice and enforce the actual clamp conditions \(w(0)=w'(0)=0\):

\[EI w'=P(Lx-x^2/2),\]
\[EI w=P(Lx^2/2-x^3/6),\]
\[w(L)=PL^3/(3EI).\]

The slope and displacement constants follow from compatibility at the support. The zero moment at the free tip follows independently from the load on the right-hand portion. Plane sections can also remain plane under pure bending of a short specimen, but shear deformation near loads and supports can matter in short cantilevers.

Worked example

Calculate a tip reading and maximum fibre strain

A rectangular steel strip has \(L=0.200\) m, \(b=20.0\) mm, \(h=8.00\) mm and \(E=200\) GPa. Depth \(h\) lies along the deflection direction, so \(I=bh^3/12=8.53\times10^{-10}\) m⁴. A \(10.0\) N tip load produces \(w(L)=0.156\) mm. The clamp moment is \(2.00\) N m; its curvature \(0.0117\) m⁻¹ gives outer-fibre strain magnitude \((0.00400)(0.0117)=46.9\) microstrain. The top is tensile and the bottom compressive for the stated load.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Vary the span to distinguish two sources of compliance

On the same strip double the free span to \(0.400\) m. Bending predicts eight times the displacement, or \(1.25\) mm; outer-fibre strain doubles to \(93.8\) microstrain. A nearly span-independent displacement offset would instead suggest motion of the clamp fixture, and a shear-displacement component at fixed load and section scales only linearly with span. The different scalings let two displacement readings test the assumptions behind a bending-only interpretation.

OneSimulation laboratory

Where does a three-dimensional beam depart from bending theory?

A 120 mm aluminium beam is clamped over its entire left end face. The free face carries a downward resultant of 30 N distributed uniformly. Keep the width at 18 mm and Young’s modulus at 70 GPa. Change only the height. The experiment asks how far a one-dimensional bending law can predict a three-dimensional displacement.

Make the paper prediction. Take downward deflection as positive. A cut at distance \(x\) from the clamp has bending-moment magnitude \(F(L-x)\). Using the rectangular section’s \(I=bh^3/12\) and integrating from the clamp gives

\[EIw'(x)=F(Lx-x^2/2),\]
\[EIw(x)=F(Lx^2/2-x^3/6),\]
\[w(L)=\frac{4FL^3}{Ebh^3}.\]

The integration constants vanish because both displacement and rotation are fixed at the clamp. Doubling the height from 6 to 12 mm should cut the tip deflection to one eighth. Calculate the 12 mm prediction, then select the two heights in the figure. Move the position control to the loaded end to read the measured ratio.

Download this run (CSV)

Explain the discrepancy. The 12 mm beam gives 0.09491 mm in the solid model and 0.09524 mm from bending. The 36 mm beam gives 0.003717 and 0.003527 mm, a difference of about 5.4% relative to the beam prediction. The solid model permits transverse shear and constrains the whole end face. Both assumptions differ from beam theory. Changing axial mesh density tests one numerical error source. It cannot by itself identify the physical origin of every difference.

Try a fourth section. Keep the force fixed at 30 N. Predict the result for an 18 mm height, run the model, then repeat with --cells 36 to check the effect of axial refinement on the centreline. The CSV reports downward displacements in millimetres.

python cantilever.py --height 18

Test a beam reduction against a solid in OneSimulation

A 120 mm cantilever of width 18 mm and modulus 70 GPa carries 30 N uniformly distributed over its end face. Make a prediction before using the slider. If the height changes from 12 to 24 mm, which changes more, the section area or its bending stiffness?

The end moment about a section a distance \(x\) from the clamp has magnitude \(F(L-x)\). The small-slope beam model first converts it into curvature \(w''=F(L-x)/(EI)\), then integrates twice with \(w(0)=w'(0)=0\). The browser performs both integrations on the selected grid by trapezoidal sums. The first integral is exact for linear curvature. The second integrates a quadratic slope and retains a grid error. Its reference is \(w(x)=Fx^2(3L-x)/(6EI)\).

Open this experiment online

Download the model for the same dimensions and change the parameters shown with the graph. OneSimulation discretises the three-dimensional solid with quadratic hexahedral elements and applies distributed end traction. Its CSV compares centreline deflection with the analytical curve. First fix height and refine axially. Then increase height at a converged mesh. The first experiment addresses numerical error within a model, the second probes the slender-beam assumptions. Under those assumptions doubling height reduces deflection eightfold because \(I=bh^3/12\).

When a beam is strong enough but too flexible

A tool rail may stay below its elastic limit while its cutting head moves outside a position tolerance. The maximum bending stress depends on the largest moment at one section. Deflection accumulates the effect of curvature over a length. A displacement sensor near an unloaded end can therefore move even if the nearby bending moment is zero. To predict it, identify both the point and direction of the required displacement.

Consider a straight, slender Euler–Bernoulli beam. Cross-sections remain plane and perpendicular to the deformed centreline. Take \(w\) positive upward and \(M\) positive in sagging. For small slopes the signed curvature is \(w''\), with \(\kappa=M/(EI)\). Integrate once to find the slope and once more to find the displacement. Each constant of integration has physical meaning. A clamp fixes displacement and rotation. A simple support fixes its vertical displacement while permitting rotation. At an ordinary join without a hinge, both displacement and slope are continuous.

Work through a tip reading

Clamp a beam at \(x=0\). Apply a downward point load \(P\) at \(x=a\) on a beam of total length \(L>a\). The signed moment in \(0<x<a\) is \(-P(a-x)\). For \(x>a\) the free segment has no external force and no moment. Taking constant stiffness and using the clamp conditions gives

\[EI w'(x)=-P(ax-x^2/2),\]
\[EI w(x)=-P(ax^2/2-x^3/6),\quad 0\le x\le a.\]

At the load point the displacement is \(-Pa^3/(3EI)\) and the slope is \(-Pa^2/(2EI)\). The free extension beyond it is straight but tilted. Use those values as initial conditions for the unloaded segment:

\[w(L)=w(a)+(L-a)w'(a)=-Pa^2(3L-a)/(6EI).\]

This expression correctly vanishes when the force is applied at the clamp and reduces to the standard end-load expression when \(a=L\).

Calculate both position and slope

Let \(EI=8400\,\mathrm{N\,m^2}\), \(L=1.20\) m, \(a=0.80\) m and \(P=100\) N. Under the load the downward displacement is 2.03 mm and the rotation magnitude is

\[100(0.80)^2/[2(8400)]=0.00381\,\mathrm{rad}.\]

The 0.40 m overhang contributes another 1.52 mm at the tip, for a total downward reading of 3.56 mm. The slope is small compared with a radian, consistent with the linear curvature assumption. A gauge at the load and one at the tip would give different stiffness estimates if their positions were omitted.

Obtain one displacement directly

There is another way to compute a single sensor reading. Introduce a fictitious force \(Q\) at the sensor in its desired positive direction. The moment becomes \(M_P+Qm\), where \(m\) is the moment per unit sensor force. The bending energy is

\[U=\int(M_P+Qm)^2/(2EI)\,dx.\]

Differentiating with respect to \(Q\) gives the displacement conjugate to that force. Set \(Q=0\) afterwards to recover the original beam.

\[\delta=\int_0^L\frac{M_P(x)m(x)}{E(x)I(x)}\,dx.\]

For a downward unit force at the free tip, \(m=-(L-x)\). Only the portion up to \(a\) contributes because \(M_P=0\) further along. The integral gives \(Pa^2(3L-a)/(6EI)\), the positive downward reading obtained above. This form is especially useful for stepped sections: split the integral where either stiffness or loading changes. Its positive self-flexibility for a load and a sensor pointing the same way is a useful sign check.

Exchange the load and the gauge

The unit-load formula supplies a test that can be made on the same beam without knowing its exact stiffness distribution. Put the same 100 N force at the tip and move the displacement sensor to \(x=0.80\) m. The new integral has exactly the previous pair of moment fields with their roles exchanged. Since their product is unchanged, the new reading is also 3.56 mm downwards. This Maxwell–Betti reciprocity applies to these work-conjugate forces and displacements in a linear elastic structure with unchanged restraints. A slipping clamp, a support that lifts off or motion large enough to change the load geometry can cause discrepancies between reciprocal tests. Swapping load and sensor positions is therefore also a practical check of the assumed support behaviour.

Quantify the deformation omitted by a bending-only model

The straight-normal assumption of Euler–Bernoulli theory omits transverse shear distortion. For a slender beam this is often small, but for a deep beam or a material with a small effective shear modulus it can dominate a displacement measurement. In a simple Timoshenko description let \(\kappa_s GA\) be the effective shear stiffness. The dimensionless shear correction factor \(\kappa_s\) accounts for the nonuniform cross-sectional shear field when a single transverse shear resultant \(V\) is used. For the illustration use \(\kappa_s=5/6\) for a homogeneous rectangular section.

For an end-loaded cantilever the bending moment magnitude is \(P(L-x)\) and the shear force magnitude is \(P\) throughout the span. Add the elastic shear energy to the bending energy. Differentiating with respect to the end force yields the total deflection under that force:

\[U=\int_0^L\left[\frac{P^2(L-x)^2}{2EI}+\frac{P^2}{2\kappa_sGA}\right]dx,\]
\[\delta=\frac{PL^3}{3EI}+\frac{PL}{\kappa_sGA}.\]

The shear contribution grows linearly with span while the bending contribution grows cubically. Their ratio is \(3EI/(\kappa_s GA L^2)\). Writing \(I/A=h^2/12\) for a rectangle gives \([E/(4\kappa_sG)](h/L)^2\). This form identifies both the geometric slenderness and material stiffness ratio required for a sensible model choice. A fixed rule based on span-to-depth alone can be poor for a sandwich section with a compliant core.

Interpret a short cantilever stiffness test

An aluminium fixture projects 120 mm from a rigid clamp. Its cross-section is 20 mm wide and 40 mm deep in the bending direction. Take \(E=70{,}000\,\mathrm{MPa}\), \(G=26{,}000\,\mathrm{MPa}\), and an end load of 500 N. The area is 800 mm² and the bending second moment is

\[20(40)^3/12=106667\,\mathrm{mm^4}.\]

In consistent N–mm units the bending deflection is

\[500(120)^3/[3(70000)(106667)]=0.0386\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The shear deflection is

\[500(120)/[(5/6)(26000)(800)]=0.00346\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

A dial indicator would measure about 0.0420 mm under these ideal assumptions.

Fitting a bending-only formula to this reading suggests an apparent modulus of about \(70(0.0386/0.0420)=64.2\,\mathrm{GPa}\). The discrepancy arises from omitted shear deformation despite a perfectly elastic material. Repeat the test at twice the overhang while keeping the force unchanged: bending deflection rises by eight, shear deflection by two. The fractional shear error falls to one quarter of its original value. Measuring several spans can therefore separate an incorrect modulus from a shear correction. Clamp compliance is another candidate in a real experiment. A clamp rotation produces a term with a different span dependence, and a gauge mounted close to the fixture can reveal that motion independently.

For equal applied loads at spans \(L\) and \(2L\), express measured compliances as \(C_1=B+S\) and \(C_2=8B+2S\), where \(B\) and \(S\) are the original bending and shear contributions per unit load. Then \(B=(C_2-2C_1)/6\) and \(S=C_1-B\). A negative inferred \(S\) signals measurement uncertainty or an omitted boundary deformation, since the assumed elastic shear energy must be positive. This inversion makes the two-span proposal a quantitative model test.

Taking three spans instead of two permits a residual check, because the third measured displacement is then predicted without adjusting either fitted contribution.

Problem sheet 2 · Beam actions and deformations

Follow each load from the support diagram to the internal moment and the sensor reading.

1. Move a wheel along a beam

A single 8 kN wheel loads a simply supported 4.0 m span 1.0 m from A. (a) Find the reactions and largest moment. (b) Place the wheel 2.0 m from A and repeat. (c) A section has \(Z=160\,000\) mm³. Compare the largest nominal stresses.

Work from reactions to stress

The first moment balance about A gives \(R_B=8(1)/4=2\) kN and \(R_A=6\) kN. Shear changes sign at the wheel, which is where \(M_{\max}=6(1)=6\) kN·m. At midspan the reactions are both 4 kN and \(M_{\max}=8\) kN·m. Convert moments to N·mm and divide by \(Z\). The extreme stresses are 37.5 and 50.0 MPa. Symmetry maximises the moment from one mobile wheel at midspan for this support arrangement.

2. Locate the maximum moment under a tapered load

A 3.0 m simple span carries downward load rising from zero at A to 6.0 kN/m at B. (a) Determine the reactions. (b) Obtain \(V(x)\) and \(M(x)\) on the open span. (c) Find the maximum and check the other end condition.

Integrate the triangular load

The resultant is 9 kN at 2 m from A, so \(R_B=6\) kN and \(R_A=3\) kN. In metres the intensity is \(2x\) kN/m. Integrating \(V'=-2x\) gives \(V=3-x^2\) kN. With \(M(0)=0\), \(M=3x-x^3/3\) kN·m. The positive shear vanishes at \(x=\sqrt3=1.732\) m, where \(M=2\sqrt3=3.46\) kN·m. At B, \(M(3)=9-9=0\) and the 6 kN support reaction exactly cancels the remaining negative shear.

3. Compare two displacements on a cantilever

A 0.90 m cantilever with \(EI=6000\,\mathrm{N\,m^2}\) carries a 240 N downward tip force. (a) Derive the end deflection by the unit-load method. (b) Compute the deflection at the halfway station using a fictitious load there. (c) Explain why linear interpolation from the tip is inaccurate.

Work the two virtual-load diagrams

For the tip reading multiply \(M=-P(L-x)\) by \(m=-(L-x)\) and integrate from 0 to \(L\). The displacement is

\[PL^3/(3EI)=9.72\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

For a unit load at \(a=L/2\), \(m=-(a-x)\) up to \(a\) and zero beyond. Thus

\[\delta(a)=P\int_0^a(L-x)(a-x)\,dx/(EI)=Pa^2(3L-a)/(6EI)=3.04\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Half the tip reading would be 4.86 mm. The zero slope at the clamp and bending curvature throughout the beam give a nonlinear deflection shape.

Size a symmetric sandwich beam from its bending rigidity

A 30 mm wide beam has a 16 mm core of modulus 2 GPa and two 2 mm faces of modulus 70 GPa. For a 15 N m moment, locate the neutral axis, calculate curvature and maximum face stress, and determine the fraction of bending rigidity supplied by the core.

Show the worked solution

Symmetry of materials and geometry fixes the neutral axis halfway through the 20 mm total depth. Core \(I=30(16)^3/12=10240\,\mathrm{mm^4}\). Each face centroid is 9 mm from mid-depth and contributes \(30(2)^3/12+60(9)^2=4880\,\mathrm{mm^4}\). Rigidity is

\[2000(10240)+2(70000)(4880)=7.0368\times10^8\,\mathrm{N\,mm^2}.\]

Curvature is

\[15000/D=2.132\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{mm^{-1}}.\]

Outer face stress magnitude is \(70000(10)\kappa=14.9\,\mathrm{MPa}\). The core provides only \(20.48/703.68=2.91\%\) of bending rigidity. It still keeps the faces separated and can carry substantial transverse shear.

Chapter 5 · Stress transformation and combined loading

Find principal stresses and elastic yield measures at a shaft surface carrying bending and torsion.

From first principles

Recover principal normal stresses from traction balance

For a small material tetrahedron, equilibrium of the tractions on its faces gives Cauchy’s relation \(\mathbf t(\mathbf n)=\boldsymbol\sigma\mathbf n\). In a classical continuum without body couples, local moment balance makes \(\boldsymbol\sigma\) symmetric. In plane stress select a section normal \(\mathbf n=(n_x,n_y)\) in the sheet. A principal section carries traction parallel to its normal, so it solves \(\boldsymbol\sigma\mathbf n=\lambda\mathbf n\). A nonzero normal exists only if

\[(\sigma_x-\lambda)(\sigma_y-\lambda)-\tau_{xy}^2=0 .\]

Solve the quadratic by completing the square about the mean normal stress. This gives both principal values:

\[\sigma_{1,2}=\frac{\sigma_x+\sigma_y}{2}\pm\sqrt{\left(\frac{\sigma_x-\sigma_y}{2}\right)^2+\tau_{xy}^2}.\]

The associated real eigenvectors are orthogonal when the values differ. For a pure shear, the principal stresses are equal and opposite even though normal stresses on the original coordinate cuts are zero. For a traction-free sheet the third principal value is zero; this third direction must be included when applying a three-dimensional yield criterion.

Derivation and applications

Find the plane carrying the largest shear traction

A tensile test measures a normal stress on one specified cut. In a machine part another cut through the same point can carry shear even when the original axes coincide with the loading. Assume plane stress: the free faces normal to \(z\) have zero traction and the nonzero components are \(\sigma_x,\sigma_y,\tau_{xy}\). Choose an in-plane unit normal \(\mathbf n=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\) and tangent \(\mathbf s=(-\sin\theta,\cos\theta)\). Cauchy’s traction relation gives the force per actual area on that cut:

\[\mathbf t=\boldsymbol\sigma\mathbf n=(\sigma_x\cos\theta+\tau_{xy}\sin\theta,\ \tau_{xy}\cos\theta+\sigma_y\sin\theta).\]

Project onto the normal and tangent. Introduce \(m=(\sigma_x+\sigma_y)/2\) and \(d=(\sigma_x-\sigma_y)/2\). The double-angle identities rearrange the two projections into a rotation:

\[\sigma_n-m=d\cos2\theta+\tau_{xy}\sin2\theta,\]
\[\tau_{ns}=-d\sin2\theta+\tau_{xy}\cos2\theta.\]

Squaring and adding cancels the cross terms. This supplies both the in-plane principal stresses and the range of shear traction:

\[(\sigma_n-m)^2+\tau_{ns}^2=d^2+\tau_{xy}^2=R^2,\]
\[\sigma_{1,2}=m\pm R,\]
\[|\tau_{ns}|_{\max}=R .\]

Planes of largest in-plane shear are rotated \(45^\circ\) from principal planes. The three-dimensional maximum also includes planes involving the out-of-plane principal stress, which equals zero under plane stress. That distinction matters whenever both in-plane principal stresses have the same sign.

Worked example

Resolve the stress on a particular cut

Take \((\sigma_x,\sigma_y,\tau_{xy})=(90,30,40)\) MPa. The centre and radius are \(60\) and \(50\) MPa, giving in-plane principal stresses \(110\) and \(10\) MPa. On a cut with normal at \(45^\circ\), the projections give \(\sigma_n=100\) MPa and \(\tau_{ns}=-30\) MPa. Their point lies on the circle because \(40^2+30^2=50^2\). Including the zero out-of-plane stress gives absolute maximum shear \(55\) MPa.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Test a reported shear measurement

For equal biaxial tensile stresses \(70\) MPa with \(\tau_{xy}=0\), both \(d\) and \(R\) vanish. Every in-plane cut has \(70\) MPa normal stress and zero shear. A gauge claiming \(20\) MPa shear on a plane normal entirely in the sheet contradicts this state. A plane tilted \(45^\circ\) out of the sheet can carry \(35\) MPa, since it mixes principal stresses of \(70\) and zero MPa.
