A section resultant does not specify a failure plane

The free-body diagram of a shaft gives bending moments and torque. A potential crack, however, can cut the shaft on any plane. To compare the local stress with a material test, first evaluate the stress components at the same material point. At the outer fibre of a circular shaft the bending normal stress may be large. At the neutral axis the bending stress vanishes. Values from different points cannot be combined into one stress state.

For a circular shaft of diameter \(d\) bending moment \(M\) gives surface stress \(32M/(\pi d^3)\), and torque \(T\) gives surface shear \(16T/(\pi d^3)\). The torsion relation uses \(J=\pi d^4/32\). The combination assumes elastic small deformation in a smooth prismatic portion, sufficiently far from shoulders, load introduction and keyways. At a traction-free outer surface the radial traction is zero. Use a local basis with longitudinal and circumferential axes for the remaining plane-stress components.

Change the orientation of a cut

In two dimensions write the stress tensor with normal components \(\sigma_x,\sigma_y\) and shear \(\tau_{xy}\). The traction vector on a cut with unit normal \(\mathbf n\) is \(\mathbf t=\boldsymbol\sigma\mathbf n\). Project that vector along and perpendicular to \(\mathbf n=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\). The normal stress becomes a constant average plus a sinusoidal term; the shear varies with twice the same angle:

\[\sigma_n=\frac{\sigma_x+\sigma_y}{2}+\frac{\sigma_x-\sigma_y}{2}\cos 2\theta+\tau_{xy}\sin 2\theta.\]

The largest and smallest in-plane normal stresses differ from the mean by \(R=\sqrt{[(\sigma_x-\sigma_y)/2]^2+\tau_{xy}^2}\). The shear on those principal planes vanishes. Taking the third principal stress as zero allows a three-dimensional maximum-shear check: evaluate half the difference of the largest and smallest of all three principal stresses. In a biaxial tensile state, ignoring the zero third stress can underestimate this difference.

Compare principal stresses on a machine shaft

Let a 40 mm diameter steel shaft carry \(M=300\) N·m and \(T=200\) N·m. At its tensile bending surface, \(\sigma_x=47.75\) MPa, \(\sigma_y=0\) and \(\tau_{xy}=15.92\) MPa. The in-plane mean is 23.87 MPa and the radius is 28.69 MPa. The principal stresses are about 52.57 and −4.82 MPa. The plane of greatest tension is rotated through half of \(\operatorname{atan2}(2\tau_{xy},\sigma_x-\sigma_y)\), here 16.8° from the original plane normal. The maximum shear magnitude is 28.7 MPa.

A useful check is the trace: the principal stresses sum to 47.75 MPa, the same sum as the original normal components. A second check is the determinant: the two principal values have opposite signs since the determinant of this particular matrix is \(-\tau_{xy}^2\). A picture with the two directions marked prevents confusing the angle of a plane normal with that of the line drawn for the plane itself.

Interpret an equivalent stress

For isotropic ductile materials a common onset-of-yield model uses von Mises equivalent stress. Under plane stress it is \(\sqrt{\sigma_x^2-\sigma_x\sigma_y+\sigma_y^2+3\tau_{xy}^2}\), giving 55.1 MPa here. Compare this scalar with the uniaxial yield strength from a material test under a comparable temperature and loading rate. A given yield strength of 220 MPa corresponds to a nominal static factor of about 4.0. That number answers a defined static question. A shaft rotating under a stationary transverse force cycles the tensile and compressive stress of a given material point once per revolution. Evaluating its fatigue resistance requires the stress range and a separate material model.

Change the mean stress without changing the distortion

Apply a uniform external hydrostatic pressure of 50 MPa to a material point whose original principal stresses are 100, 0 and 0 MPa. The new principal stresses are 50, −50 and −50 MPa, since pressure acts on every plane. Their pairwise differences remain 100, 0 and −100 MPa, so the von Mises stress remains 100 MPa. This illustrates why a distortion-based yield condition must include all three normal directions under confinement. Applying a plane-stress formula after adding pressure only to the in-plane directions would represent a different three-dimensional loading.

Derive the two membrane stresses of a thin cylinder

A hydraulic accumulator behaves differently in its axial and circumferential directions even if it is made of isotropic steel. Consider a long cylindrical shell of mean radius \(r\), wall thickness \(t\ll r\), closed ends and internal gauge pressure \(p\). Away from nozzles and end transitions, symmetry permits a membrane model with tensile stresses uniform through the thickness. The radial stress changes from approximately \(-p\) at the inner wall to zero outside. Its magnitude is small compared with \(pr/t\) if the shell is thin.

Cut the cylinder longitudinally into two halves of length \(L\). The net force from pressure acts on the projected rectangle of area \(2rL\), not on the curved wetted area. The two cut edges carry hoop tension. A different cut across the full circular end isolates the cap thrust and the single axial membrane stress. The resulting balances are

\[2\sigma_\theta tL=p(2rL),\]
\[2\pi rt\sigma_z=p\pi r^2,\]
\[\sigma_\theta=pr/t,\]
\[\sigma_z=pr/(2t).\]

The factor of two follows from geometry and end load transmission. If the ends are open and pressure thrust returns through a separate frame, the cylinder may carry little axial membrane force. Always follow the cap force into the structure before assigning axial stress. A cap anchored externally and a welded pressure shell have different load paths even at the same gauge pressure.

Infer shell strain and the effect of axial restraint

For a closed vessel with \(r=0.20\,\mathrm m\), \(t=4.0\,\mathrm{mm}\) and \(p=1.2\,\mathrm{MPa}\), the membrane stresses are 60 MPa circumferentially and 30 MPa axially. These directions are principal at an unperturbed surface. Under plane stress the von Mises stress is

\[\sqrt{60^2+30^2-60(30)}=52.0\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

With \(E=200\,\mathrm{GPa}\) and \(\nu=0.30\), an outer hoop gauge reads \((60-0.30\times30)/200000=255\) microstrain and an axial gauge reads only \((30-0.30\times60)/200000=60\) microstrain. The radius increases by about \(0.20(255\times10^{-6})=51\,\mathrm{\mu m}\). The smaller axial signal is particularly sensitive to temperature correction.

Now imagine an axial restraint strong enough to hold the length constant without affecting the radius directly. The condition \(\varepsilon_z=0\) gives

\[\sigma_z=\nu\sigma_\theta=18\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

The cap thrust has not disappeared. An external axial reaction must remove the difference of 12 MPa integrated over the shell area \(2\pi rt\), about 60.3 kN. A gauge-based interpretation using the unrestrained 30 MPa estimate would miss this load. Conversely, where the restraint attaches, local bending and stress concentration may dominate the smooth membrane solution. Locate comparison gauges several characteristic wall dimensions away from such load introduction.

The force calculation can be audited directly. Pressure thrust on the end cap is \(1.2\pi(200)^2=151\,\mathrm{kN}\) in MPa and millimetres. An 18 MPa shell stress carries

\[18[2\pi(200)(4)]=90.5\,\mathrm{kN}.\]

Their difference, 60.3 kN, is precisely the required external load path. A restraint instrumented with a load cell would test the assumed axial boundary condition independently of the strain readings.

Extend the traction balance to three dimensions

In three dimensions a cut normal has three components. Cauchy’s relation remains \(\mathbf t=\boldsymbol\sigma\mathbf n\). Project onto the unit normal to obtain signed normal stress, then remove that projection to obtain the shear traction. Rotating a diagram does not rotate the tensor physically; changing its components does.

\[\sigma_n=\mathbf n^T\boldsymbol\sigma\mathbf n,\]
\[\mathbf t_s=\boldsymbol\sigma\mathbf n-\sigma_n\mathbf n.\]

The eigenvectors give principal cut directions with zero shear traction. The following notebook evaluates the symmetric eigensystem and independently verifies both the tensor reconstruction and the orthogonality of each shear traction to its cut normal.

Interactive calculation · Inspect three-dimensional traction on every cut

Change the out-of-plane shear to leave the original plane-stress example. Rotate the sphere of cut normals, then tilt the plotted slice through it. The force segments use a common symbolic scale; they show traction directions, not displacements.

Open this live calculation

OneSimulation notebook · solved spatial stresses, code and output

The code solves the illustrated stress model with OneSimulation and checks its equilibrium or eigensystem. The figure is the output of that code.

"""Compute principal stresses with OneSimulation and show Cauchy traction in 3D."""
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from onesim.numerics.linear.matrices.dense_linear_algebra import symmetric_eigensystem

# Symmetric Cauchy tensor in MPa; edit the entries to reproduce the browser controls.
sigma=np.array([[80.,30.,15.],[30.,20.,0.],[15.,0.,-30.]])
principal, axes = symmetric_eigensystem(sigma)
assert np.allclose(axes@np.diag(principal)@axes.T,sigma)
assert np.allclose(axes.T@axes,np.eye(3))
print('Principal stresses [MPa]:',np.round(principal,5))
print('Absolute maximum shear [MPa]:',round((principal[-1]-principal[0])/2,5))
phi=np.linspace(0,2*np.pi,12,endpoint=False)
theta=np.linspace(.25,np.pi-.25,6)
n=np.array([[np.sin(t)*np.cos(p),np.sin(t)*np.sin(p),np.cos(t)] for t in theta for p in phi])
traction=n@sigma.T
normal=np.sum(n*traction,axis=1)
shear=traction-normal[:,None]*n
assert np.max(abs(np.sum(n*shear,axis=1)))<1e-12
assert normal.min()>=principal[0]-1e-10 and normal.max()<=principal[-1]+1e-10
fig=plt.figure(figsize=(9,4.2),layout='constrained');ax=fig.add_subplot(121,projection='3d')
ax.quiver(*n.T,*(traction/120).T,length=.5,normalize=False,color='#19596a',linewidth=.7)
ax.scatter(*n.T,c=normal,cmap='coolwarm',s=12,vmin=-100,vmax=100)
for j in range(3):ax.plot(*np.array([-axes[:,j],axes[:,j]]).T,label=f'{principal[j]:.1f} MPa')
ax.set(xlabel='Normal x',ylabel='Normal y',zlabel='Normal z',title='Traction vectors on unit-normal sphere')
ax.set_box_aspect((1,1,1));ax.legend(fontsize=7)
a=np.linspace(0,2*np.pi,361);incline=np.deg2rad(30)
directions=np.column_stack((np.cos(a),np.sin(a)*np.cos(incline),np.sin(a)*np.sin(incline)))
t=directions@sigma.T;sn=np.sum(t*directions,axis=1);st=np.linalg.norm(t-sn[:,None]*directions,axis=1)
ax2=fig.add_subplot(122);ax2.plot(np.rad2deg(a),sn,label='Normal stress');ax2.plot(np.rad2deg(a),st,label='Shear magnitude')
ax2.set(xlabel='Direction angle in tilted plane (degrees)',ylabel='Stress (MPa)',title='A slice through the directional response');ax2.legend()
fig.savefig('complete-engineering-traction3d.png',dpi=170);plt.show()

Contact pressure between elastic cylinders · Stress, deformation and structural measurement

Two polished cylindrical rollers touch along a narrow strip. Dividing load by a nominal full face area gives little indication of the actual pressure. Use the local shape of the gap and elastic half-space compliance to obtain the pressure distribution, and decide whether a measured contact width can determine material stiffness.

Chapter 6 · Elastic stability of compression members

Derive the ideal buckling load, check its governing axis and explain how an imperfection changes the measured response.

From first principles

Derive Euler’s pinned-column load from transverse equilibrium

Take a straight slender column of length \(L\) with pins at both ends, constant rigidity \(EI\) and centred compressive load \(P>0\). Assume a small lateral displacement \(w(x)\), linear elastic material and negligible shear deformation. With no external transverse load, the axial force acting through the end line has a moment arm equal to the lateral displacement. With a consistent bending sign convention this gives the homogeneous equilibrium equation

\[EI w''+Pw=0,\]
\[w(0)=w(L)=0.\]

A perfectly straight shape solves this equation for every load. To find when a neighbouring bent equilibrium becomes possible, solve for a nonzero amplitude. The first pin removes the cosine term; the far pin then selects discrete wave numbers:

\[w=A\sin(\beta x),\]
\[\beta^2=P/(EI),\]
\[\sin(\beta L)=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad P_n=n^2\pi^2EI/L^2 .\]

The lowest eigenvalue is the first elastic buckling load within this ideal model. Buckling concerns loss of stiffness to lateral perturbations. Material yielding, crookedness and other end conditions can change a measured threshold even when the axial stress is still nominally uniform.

Background · Euler load
\[P_{\rm cr}=\pi^2 EI/L_{\rm eff}^2\]

The ideal slender elastic column buckles in its lowest admissible mode. Support conditions determine effective length and change the result quadratically.

Derivation and applications

When does a continuous lateral support change the buckling mode?

A slender compressed member may rest on an elastic bed rather than being unsupported between pins. The axial compression lowers the energy of lateral deflection; bending stiffness and the support both raise it. Take a straight, prismatic Euler–Bernoulli column with span \(L\), bending rigidity \(EI\) and compressive dead load \(P>0\). Let a distributed support supply restoring force per length \(-k_f w\), proportional to transverse displacement \(w\). Neglect imperfections, shear deflection and nonlinearities beyond second-order potential energy.

For small slopes the ends approach by \(\int w'^2dx/2\). The change of potential energy therefore contains three contributions:

\[\Delta\Pi=\tfrac12\int_0^L[EI(w'')^2+k_f w^2-P(w')^2]\,dx .\]

With pins at both ends take \(w=A\sin(n\pi x/L)\), which satisfies zero deflection and zero moment there. For uniform properties these are independent eigenfunctions. With \(\beta_n=n\pi/L\), each squared sine or cosine integrates to \(L/2\), giving

\[\Delta\Pi_n=\tfrac14LA^2(EI\beta_n^4+k_f-P\beta_n^2).\]

At the threshold the coefficient of \(A^2\) first vanishes. Dividing by \(\beta_n^2\) gives a candidate compression for each mode; the physically relevant first threshold is their minimum:

\[P_n=EI\beta_n^2+k_f/\beta_n^2,\]
\[P_{\rm cr}=\min_{n\ge1}P_n .\]

A long-wavelength deflection costs little bending energy but displaces the elastic bed strongly for a prescribed end shortening. Short wavelengths avoid that displacement at the expense of large curvature. For \(k_f=0\) the first sine mode recovers Euler’s pinned-column result. A sufficiently stiff bed can instead favor a mode having more than one half-wave.

Worked example

Find the first threshold for a supported strip

Take \(L=\pi\) m, \(EI=100\) N m² and \(k_f=900\) N/m². Then \(\beta_n=n\) m⁻¹ and \(P_1=1000\) N, \(P_2=625\) N, \(P_3=1000\) N. For larger \(n\) the bending term alone exceeds \(625\) N. The first instability is therefore the two-half-wave shape at \(625\) N, even though the ends are still ordinary pins.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Specify a bed stiffness at the mode exchange

For this same strip increase \(k_f\) from zero. Equating \(P_1\) and \(P_2\) gives \(EI\beta_1^2+k_f/\beta_1^2=4EI\beta_1^2+k_f/(4\beta_1^2)\), hence \(k_f=4EI\beta_1^4=400\) N/m². Both modes then have \(P=500\) N. Raising the support stiffness increases both candidate loads but changes which one is lowest. A calculation checking only the first shape would overpredict the threshold beyond this exchange.

Equilibrium can persist while stability changes

Press gently on the ends of a thin steel ruler. The straight configuration satisfies force equilibrium over a range of compressive loads. Eventually a small lateral disturbance produces a large deflection. The issue concerns the restoring response to a disturbance, even when the nominal compressive stress is well within the material’s elastic range.

Model a slender, initially straight, uniform elastic column with ideal pins at both ends. Apply a conservative compressive load \(P\) along the initial end-to-end line, with small transverse slope and negligible shear deformation. A lateral excursion \(w(x)\) creates a bending moment of magnitude \(P|w|\). Combining its restoring sign with the small-curvature law gives

\[EI w''+P w=0.\]

The difference from a laterally loaded beam is that the load itself creates a moment through the unknown displacement.

Apply two pin conditions

Write \(k^2=P/(EI)\) and integrate:

\[w=A\sin(kx)+B\cos(kx).\]

The first pin imposes \(w(0)=0\), so \(B=0\). The second imposes \(A\sin(kL)=0\). The zero-amplitude straight solution always exists. A nonzero amplitude is possible within this linearised model only when \(kL=n\pi\), for positive integer \(n\). The smallest compressive load admitting a bent mode is

\[P_{\mathrm{cr}}=\frac{\pi^2EI}{L^2},\]
\[w_1(x)\propto\sin(\pi x/L).\]

The proportionality leaves the amplitude undetermined. It locates a loss of stiffness, not a post-buckled deflection. The same result follows from energy: for \(w=A\sin(\pi x/L)\) the quadratic elastic energy is \(EI\pi^4A^2/(4L^3)\), while end shortening releases \(P\pi^2A^2/(4L)\). Their difference changes sign at the critical load.

Find which direction buckles first

A rectangular steel strut is 1.50 m long with section 40 mm by 12 mm and \(E=200\) GPa. Bending across the 12 mm dimension gives

\[I_{\min}=40(12)^3/12=5760\,\mathrm{mm^4}.\]

Across the 40 mm dimension,

\[I=12(40)^3/12=64\,000\,\mathrm{mm^4}.\]

Free to buckle either way, the column chooses the smaller bending stiffness. Converting \(5760\,\mathrm{mm^4}\) to \(5.76\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm{m^4}\) gives \(P_{\rm cr}=5.05\) kN.

The corresponding mean stress is only \(5050/480=10.5\) MPa. The radius of gyration about the weak axis is

\[r_g=\sqrt{I/A}=3.46\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Writing \(P_{\rm cr}/A=\pi^2E/(L/r_g)^2\) makes the sensitivity to slenderness explicit. If a real brace prevents weak-axis deflection at midspan, a pin-like brace divides that buckling length by two. Check other unbraced axes and the brace stiffness before crediting the fourfold ideal increase.

Interpret a pre-curved strut

Suppose the unloaded column already bows with amplitude \(w_0\) in the first sine shape. Elastic bending resists the curvature change from that initial shape. Writing its total amplitude as \(w\), the mode equation now gives \(P_{\rm cr}(w-w_0)=Pw\), hence \(w=w_0/(1-P/P_{\rm cr})\). If \(w_0=1.0\) mm, the predicted amplitude is 2.0 mm at half the ideal load and 5.0 mm at 80%. The steep increase can show up in a test well before a distinct peak load.

This model assumes small slopes and a material still in its elastic range. Large deflection changes the geometry, and local yielding can reduce the tangent stiffness. End restraint also changes the eigenvalue. The often-used effective-length notation replaces \(L\) by the pin-ended length giving the same ideal load. It must reflect the actual translations and rotations permitted by the supports.

Use two readings to estimate an elastic critical load

For an imperfect elastic column dominated by its first buckling shape, write total offset \(e=e_0/(1-P/P_{\rm cr})\). Let \(y=e-e_0\) be the additional deflection measured from its unloaded shape. Rearrangement gives

\[y/P=(y+e_0)/P_{\rm cr}.\]

A graph of \(y/P\) against \(y\) should therefore be linear with slope \(1/P_{\rm cr}\). As a consistency test, measured pairs \(P=20\) kN, \(y=0.50\) mm and \(P=30\) kN, \(y=1.00\) mm give slope \((1/30-0.5/20)/(1.0-0.5)=1/60\,\mathrm{kN^{-1}}\). The implied critical load is 60 kN and the intercept implies a 1 mm initial offset. Clearance or nonlinear material response can spoil this inference before the theoretical critical load is reached.

Estimate a buckling load from an assumed lateral shape

The differential buckling equation is especially simple for uniform pinned columns. An energy method extends the calculation to cases where stiffness varies or extra bracing is present. Take a conservative compressive dead load \(P\) acting along the original axis. A small lateral displacement \(w(x)\) stores bending energy \(\tfrac12\int EI(w'')^2dx\). To the same quadratic order in slope the ends move towards one another by \(\tfrac12\int(w')^2dx\), allowing the axial load to do positive work. The straight configuration loses stiffness against a given shape when these quadratic contributions balance.

\[P_R=\frac{\int_0^L EI(w'')^2dx}{\int_0^L(w')^2dx}.\]

The trial shape must satisfy the geometric support conditions and must possess finite bending energy. Its amplitude cancels, since numerator and denominator are both quadratic in the same displacement. For this conservative model the smallest possible quotient over all admissible shapes equals the fundamental buckling load. A particular admissible shape generally yields an upper estimate. This is a potential hazard in assessment: a plausible trial shape can overestimate the true capacity and refinement should make the estimate decrease.

For the pinned uniform column choose \(w=A\sin(\pi x/L)\). Squaring and integrating the derivatives recovers \(P_R=EI\pi^2/L^2\). A deliberately less accurate trial \(w=A x(L-x)\) has constant curvature and gives

\[P_R=12EI/L^2.\]

It satisfies zero displacement at both pins, hence is admissible in this energy calculation, but it misses the exact sinusoidal curvature. Its predicted load is about 22% high.

Decide whether a central brace can suppress the lowest mode

Install a lateral spring of stiffness \(k\) at the midpoint of a 2.0 m pinned member with \(EI=40\,\mathrm{kN\,m^2}\). The spring adds \(\tfrac12 k w(L/2)^2\) to stored energy. Using the first sine shape gives

\[P_R=\pi^2 EI/L^2+2kL/\pi^2.\]

Its unbraced value is 98.7 kN. For \(k=100\,\mathrm{kN/m}\), the quotient rises by \(2(100)(2)/\pi^2=40.5\) kN, giving 139.2 kN. The value estimates the improved symmetric mode; the spring generally changes that mode’s shape.

The second sine shape has a node at midspan, so its frequency-independent elastic buckling load remains \(4\pi^2EI/L^2=394.8\,\mathrm{kN}\). Equating the two trial values would suggest a transition near \(k=731\,\mathrm{kN/m}\). This crossing identifies when a one-shape description merits scrutiny. It cannot prove the actual transition because the symmetric trial result is an upper bound. A brace added at midspan can suppress a large single bow and still leave a two-half-wave buckling mode essentially unaffected. Checking the other bending direction and the brace’s own connection stiffness is essential before applying either idealised improvement to a real member.

A midspan dial indicator responds strongly to the symmetric shape and sees zero displacement for the ideal two-half-wave shape. An experiment observing only that indicator might report successful suppression even as the second mode grows. A second indicator at quarter span has nonzero response to both shapes and reveals the change of governing mechanism.

Problem sheet 3 · Strength and stability

Use point stress for a material check and structural stiffness for a buckling check.

1. Change shaft speed at constant power

A smooth solid shaft of diameter 30 mm transmits 6 kW at 1200 rpm and carries a fixed bending moment of 80 N·m. (a) Find the torque and surface stresses. (b) Compute von Mises stress. (c) Repeat at 600 rpm at the same power and explain which stress changes.

Combine bending and torsion

At 1200 rpm the angular speed is \(40\pi\) rad/s and \(T=6000/(40\pi)=47.75\) N·m. At the tensile surface \(\sigma=32(80)/(\pi d^3)=30.18\) MPa and \(\tau=16(47.75)/(\pi d^3)=9.01\) MPa. Thus

\[\sigma_{\rm vm}=\sqrt{\sigma^2+3\tau^2}=33.98\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

Halving the speed doubles the torque and the shear to 18.01 MPa while bending stays fixed. The equivalent stress becomes about 43.4 MPa. Keeping power constant does not keep the torque demand constant.

2. Decide which plane controls a strut

A straight pin-ended column is 2.0 m long with \(E=200\) GPa, \(I_y=8.0\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{m^4}\) and \(I_z=2.0\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm{m^4}\). (a) Calculate both mode loads. (b) Add an ideal midspan lateral brace that halves only the effective length for bending about \(y\). Determine the controlling load. (c) Compare your result with a prediction multiplying the original capacity by four.

Compare both elastic modes

Using \(P=\pi^2EI/L^2\) gives 39.5 kN about \(y\) and 98.7 kN about \(z\); initially the first governs. After bracing, the ideal \(y\) load is 158 kN. The \(z\) load is still 98.7 kN and now governs. The ideal capacity therefore rises by a factor 2.5, because the weak mode switches. The brace must restrain displacement in the direction in which the original weak mode bends.

3. Interpret a lateral-displacement reading

A test column has initial sinusoidal bow 0.40 mm. At a compressive load of 12 kN the total lateral amplitude is 1.00 mm. (a) Infer its first-mode elastic critical load. (b) Estimate amplitude at 16 kN. (c) If the area is 300 mm² and \(Z=1200\) mm³, estimate the most compressive nominal stress at 16 kN.

Infer the stiffness loss and add the bending stress

From \(w_0/w=1-P/P_{\rm cr}\), the 12 kN reading gives

\[P_{\rm cr}=12/(1-0.4)=20\,\mathrm{kN}.\]

At 16 kN the predicted amplitude is

\[0.40/(1-16/20)=2.0\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The direct stress is

\[16\,000/300=53.3\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

The additional moment is \(16\,000(2.0)=32\,000\) N·mm, giving \(M/Z=26.7\) MPa. At the more compressed fibre the two effects add, reaching about 80.0 MPa. The inference assumes an elastic member, a first-mode imperfection and small slopes.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Stress transformation and combined loading; Elastic stability of compression members. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 7 · Torsional stiffness of shafts and closed sections

Derive torque–twist relations and explain the large difference in stiffness between an intact tube and an open strip.

From first principles

Derive the torque–twist relation from local shear deformation

Consider a straight circular shaft in small elastic torsion. In Saint-Venant torsion of a circular section, each cross-section can rotate as a rigid plane relative to the next. Let its rotation be \(\phi(x)\) and take two sections separated by \(dx\). At radius \(r\) their relative tangential displacement is \(r\,d\phi\). Dividing by the axial spacing gives engineering shear strain and, through the measured shear modulus \(G\), shear stress:

\[\gamma_{x\theta}=r\,d\phi/dx,\]
\[\tau_{x\theta}=Gr\,d\phi/dx .\]

Each patch of area \(dA\) carries tangential force \(\tau\,dA\) with moment arm \(r\). Sum these moments over the section and integrate the resulting twist rate along the shaft:

\[T=\int_A r\tau\,dA=GJ\phi',\]
\[J=\int_A r^2\,dA,\]
\[\Delta\phi=\int \frac{T(x)}{G(x)J(x)}dx .\]

For a homogeneous solid circular shaft \(J=\pi R^4/2\), so doubling radius strongly increases torsional stiffness. Noncircular sections generally warp and their torsion constant need not equal this polar area moment. The circular-shaft kinematics should not be used unchanged for an open thin-walled section.

Derivation and applications

How two bonded shaft materials share one applied torque

Two shafts in series carry the same torque; a bonded core and sleeve instead undergo the same twist per length. Consider a circular core of radius \(a\) and shear modulus \(G_c\), bonded to a concentric sleeve with outer radius \(b\) and modulus \(G_s\). Use a long prismatic region away from load introduction, small elastic deformations, perfect bond and no interfacial slip. Let \(\phi(x)\) describe section rotation. Two cross-sections separated by \(dx\) acquire a relative tangential motion \(r\,d\phi\) at radius \(r\):

\[\gamma_{x\theta}=r\phi',\]
\[\tau_i(r)=G_i r\phi' .\]

Compatibility makes \(\phi'\) common. The shear stress may change at the material boundary because the moduli differ. The torque contribution from a thin annulus is its area \(2\pi r\,dr\) times shear stress times radius. Integrating in each material gives

\[T_c=G_cJ_c\phi',\]
\[T_s=G_sJ_s\phi',\]
\[J_c=\frac{\pi a^4}{2},\]
\[J_s=\frac{\pi(b^4-a^4)}{2}.\]

The applied torque \(T\) is their sum. Solving first for the common twist and then for the load split yields

\[\phi'=\frac{T}{G_cJ_c+G_sJ_s},\]
\[\frac{T_c}{T}=\frac{G_cJ_c}{G_cJ_c+G_sJ_s}.\]

The denominator has units of N m². Setting both shear moduli equal recovers the homogeneous solid shaft independently of the arbitrary division at \(a\). If the sleeve is unbonded and torque is applied only to the core, equal twist is no longer a valid compatibility condition.

Worked example

Predict the twist of a reinforced shaft

Take \(a=10.0\) mm, \(b=15.0\) mm, \(G_c=80.0\) GPa and \(G_s=30.0\) GPa. The rigidities are \(1257\) and \(1914\) N m². Under \(T=60.0\) N m the twist rate is \(0.0189\) rad/m; over \(0.500\) m the end rotates \(0.00946\) rad. The core carries \(23.8\) N m and the sleeve \(36.2\) N m. Their stresses at \(a\) are \(15.1\) and \(5.68\) MPa respectively despite equal shear strain there.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Infer a sleeve modulus from a rotation test

A second shaft with the same dimensions and core rotates through \(0.0100\) rad over \(0.500\) m under \(50.0\) N m. The measured total rigidity is \(TL/\phi=2500\) N m². Subtracting the known core leaves \(1243\) N m². Since \(J_s=6.38\times10^{-8}\) m⁴, \(G_s=19.5\) GPa. The test would yield an apparent modulus if compliance of the grips were included in the measured rotation.

How much torque does the sleeve actually carry?

A steel core and an aluminium sleeve extend the full length of a circular shaft. They are bonded along their cylindrical interface, and the end fittings transmit the resultant torque to the complete section. A student dividing the torque in proportion to cross-sectional area obtains a plausible answer that misses both lever arm and material stiffness. We can reconstruct the division from the displacement at the interface before writing any torque balance.

Let \(a\) denote the core radius and \(b\) the outside radius. A cross section rotating by angle \(\varphi(x)\) moves a point at radius \(r\) tangentially by \(r\varphi\). Over a short axial interval the resulting engineering shear strain is \(\gamma_{x\theta}=r\,d\varphi/dx\). Bonding requires a common twist per length \(\varphi'\) for the two materials. The shear on a cross-sectional end face is \(G r\varphi'\), so it can jump at \(r=a\) even though the strain there is continuous. It is the traction on a radial-normal interface that must equilibrate on opposite sides of the bond. Confusing it with the traction on the shaft end face would wrongly force the plotted shear stresses to coincide.

The torque on an annular element is the shear times its area times its lever arm. Integration of \(r(Gr\varphi')(2\pi r\,dr)\) gives

\[T=[G_cJ_c+G_sJ_s]\varphi',\]
\[J_c=\pi a^4/2,\]
\[J_s=\pi(b^4-a^4)/2.\]

Use \(a=12\) mm, \(b=20\) mm, \(G_c=79\) GPa and \(G_s=27\) GPa. Under 100 N m the core carries 30.35 N m and the sleeve 69.65 N m. A 600 mm length rotates by 0.405 degrees. Immediately inside the interface the end-face shear is 11.18 MPa. On the other side of that same radius the lower modulus reduces it to 3.82 MPa; continuing outward increases it again. An accurate shaft twist cannot by itself identify the local peak stress unless the section and material distribution are known.

In the notebook, integrate the stress directly over the core and sleeve and compare the total with the imposed torque. Evaluate the two integrals separately so a numerical trapezoid does not smear the material jump. Then make the two moduli equal: the end-face shear curve becomes straight across the entire section, while the fraction of torque in the core equals \((a/b)^4\). This limit offers an independent check against a uniform solid shaft.

A separate way to check the torque split uses stored energy. In a unit length of shaft the strain energy density is \(\tau\gamma/2\). Integrating it over each annulus gives \(GJ(\varphi')^2/2\) for that material. Because the twist is common, the energy fractions equal the torque fractions. For the example the total strain energy is \(T\varphi/2\), about 0.35 J over 600 mm. If the two materials were joined end to end, the applied torque would be common and the angles of twist would add. The continuous cylindrical bond is the feature that selects the parallel arrangement used here.

At what radius fraction will the default materials divide torque exactly in half?

Work through the solution

Set \(G_c a^4=G_s(b^4-a^4)\). Rearranging gives

\[a/b=[G_s/(G_c+G_s)]^{1/4}=0.7106.\]

At \(b=20\) mm this corresponds to a core radius of about 14.2 mm. Run the notebook with the revised radius and read both shares. This calculation assumes a bonded circular shaft away from the ends; it cannot predict the local loading of a short end fitting.

Download the editable notebook for this example

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Rotate two adjacent sections

A torque meter measures twist between two stations on a shaft. To infer torque, first ask how material between the stations deforms. A straight circular shaft in uniform, small-deformation torsion has cross-sections that rotate as rigid discs. Two discs separated by \(dx\) rotate relative to one another by \(d\theta\). A fibre at radius \(r\) moves sideways by \(r\,d\theta\), giving shear strain \(\gamma=r\,d\theta/dx\). With elastic shear modulus \(G\), the shear stress increases linearly from the centre to the surface.

Each area element transmits a torque \(r\tau\,dA\). Integrating produces the polar second moment of area \(J\), whose dimensions are length to the fourth power. With constant torque and cross-section, integration along the shaft then converts twist per length into total relative angle.

\[T=\int_A r\tau\,dA=G\theta'\int_A r^2dA,\]
\[J=\frac{\pi}{2}(r_o^4-r_i^4),\]
\[\theta=\frac{TL}{GJ}.\]

Follow a torque through a stepped shaft

A 0.75 m aluminium tube of outer radius 15 mm and inner radius 12 mm transmits 45 N m. For \(G=26\,\mathrm{GPa}\), \(J=4.695\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{m^4}\). Its twist is

\[45(0.75)/(26\times10^9J)=0.02765\,\mathrm{rad}=1.58^\circ.\]

Surface shear is

\[45(0.015)/J=14.4\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

The inside surface still carries 80% of this stress. Drilling a central hole removes the least highly stressed material first.

Twist can also identify an unknown joint compliance. If an instrument measures 2.00° across this tube plus its end couplings, and all parts carry the same torque, the couplings contribute about 0.42°. Their combined torsional compliance is approximately \(0.42\pi/[180(45)]=1.6\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{rad/(N\,m)}\). Such a comparison assumes that the gauges do not include a second shaft segment and that the torque is transmitted without slip.

Carry torque around a single closed cell

For a long, thin-walled closed tube with a single cell, use the shear flow \(q=\tau t\), the force per unit length of wall. Longitudinal equilibrium makes this flow approximately constant around the perimeter if no intermediate longitudinal force is introduced. A length \(ds\) carries tangential force \(q\,ds\). Its moment about an arbitrary origin is \(q\) times twice the triangular area swept by the radius vector. Around the median wall contour this gives

\[T=2A_mq.\]

For a rectangular cell with median dimensions 80 by 40 mm, a torque of 320 N m gives

\[q=320000/[2(80)(40)]=50\,\mathrm{N/mm}.\]

A 2 mm wall carries 25 MPa while a 4 mm wall carries 12.5 MPa. A slender longitudinal slit interrupts the closed path. The constant circulating flow solution then ceases to apply. A seam that reconnects the two lips must transmit the longitudinal shear flow if the component is to recover its closed-section behaviour.

Distinguish two section properties

For a noncircular closed tube, the polar area moment and torsion constant generally differ. Compatibility around a single thin-walled cell gives

\[\theta'=T\oint ds/(Gt)/(4A_m^2).\]

Making only a short wall segment very thin substantially increases this integral. A wall segment can therefore control angle of twist even when equilibrium fixes the same shear flow everywhere around the cell.

Determine torque sharing between two closed cells

A single closed cell supports one constant circulating shear flow. In an aircraft-style box or a partitioned machine boom, adjacent cells share a web. Torque balance alone cannot tell how much flow each cell carries. The missing equation is twist compatibility: because the cells are bonded along the common web, their twist per unit length must agree. Assume a long, thin-walled prismatic box of one material carrying Saint-Venant torsion with negligible distortion. Use median-line areas and length-to-thickness ratios throughout.

Assign positive clockwise circulations \(q_1,q_2\) to both cells. They oppose each other on the common web, where physical flow becomes \(q_1-q_2\) measured in the first cell’s direction. Let \(S_1,S_2\) denote \(\int ds/t\) over the exterior walls of each cell and \(S_w\) that quantity along their common web. The torque and the two contour compatibility relations become

\[T=2A_1q_1+2A_2q_2,\]
\[2A_1G\theta'=(S_1+S_w)q_1-S_wq_2,\]
\[2A_2G\theta'=(S_2+S_w)q_2-S_wq_1 .\]

These equations express the same shear-strain compatibility that leads to \(J_t=4A_m^2/\oint ds/t\) for one cell. If both cells and exterior wall thicknesses are mirror images, symmetry requires equal circulations and zero common-web flow under pure torque. The common web may still carry transverse shear from a separate load case. A missing web flow under pure torque is therefore a property of that loading and symmetry.

Compute the stress in an internal dividing web

Take adjacent median rectangles 40 mm high, 60 and 30 mm wide, with every wall 2 mm thick. Then

\[A_1=2400,\ A_2=1200\,\mathrm{mm^2}.\]

Their three exterior walls give \(S_1=(60+40+60)/2=80\) and \(S_2=(30+40+30)/2=50\), while \(S_w=20\). Equal twist requires \((100q_1-20q_2)/2400=(70q_2-20q_1)/1200\), giving \(140q_1=160q_2\). Thus \(q_2=0.875q_1\), larger than one might expect from the smaller area alone.

Applying \(T=345\,\mathrm{N\,m}=345000\,\mathrm{N\,mm}\) gives \(345000=4800q_1+2400(0.875)q_1\), so \(q_1=50.0\) and \(q_2=43.75\,\mathrm{N/mm}\). The dividing web carries only 6.25 N/mm, or 3.13 MPa. The primary exterior walls carry 25.0 and 21.9 MPa. Using \(G=27000\,\mathrm{MPa}\), either contour gives \(\theta'=(100\times50-20\times43.75)/(2\times2400\times27000)=3.18\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{rad/mm}\), about 1.82° per metre. Checking the other contour independently catches a reversed sign on the shared wall. If a weld along that wall fails, equal twist and the closed paths that led to these flows can no longer be assumed.

Individual cell torque contributions are informative when interpreting a section cut. The larger cell contributes \(2(2400)(50)=240\,\mathrm{N\,m}\), and the smaller contributes \(2(1200)(43.75)=105\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). Their 345 N m sum is independent of the coordinate origin. These are bookkeeping circulations rather than two torques applied independently to separate tubes. Cutting through the common web changes the compatibility and available flow paths, so using the original partition after a cut is not justified. Surface strain measurements on the upper exterior walls and the shared web would distinguish the predicted 50, 43.75 and 6.25 N/mm flows more effectively than measuring a single outer-wall strain alone.

A comparison with a thin unpartitioned box is possible without changing outer dimensions. For the outer median rectangle 90 by 40 mm, \(A_m=3600\,\mathrm{mm^2}\) and \(\oint ds/t=2(90+40)/2=130\). Its single-cell torsion constant is

\[4A_m^2/130=398769\,\mathrm{mm^4}.\]

The partitioned solution implies

\[J_t=T/(G\theta')=345000/[27000(3.183\times10^{-5})]\simeq401500\,\mathrm{mm^4}.\]

Under this specific pure torque the additional web therefore raises torsional stiffness by less than one percent. It experiences little shear because the neighbouring circulations are similar. Its contribution to transverse load sharing, panel buckling resistance or local attachment stiffness can nevertheless be important and needs separate calculations.

Move the web to the centre, producing identical cells of median width 45 mm. Pure torque then gives equal circulations, zero shear flow on the common web and precisely the single outer-cell twist in this idealisation. This limiting case is a demanding model check. A numerical section calculation that predicts double the torsional stiffness simply because there are now two cells has likely treated the common web as two independent exterior walls.

Compare a tube and a shaft at equal material area

Hold length, material, torque and cross-sectional area fixed while hollowing a circular shaft and increasing its outer radius. For radius ratio \(r\), area conservation gives

\[R/R_s=1/\sqrt{1-r^2}.\]

Substitute in \(J=\pi R^4(1-r^4)/2\) and \(\tau_{\max}=TR/J\).

Torsion in a thin-walled two-cell section · Stress, deformation and structural measurement

Two closed cells share one slender web. The applied couple gives only their combined torque, yet the common web can carry much less shear than either exterior wall. Derive the missing compatibility equation, calculate a twist and test which measurements distinguish the two circulations.

Chapter 8 · Transverse shear and connections in built-up beams

Derive shear flow from changing bending stress and determine the force transferred between parts of a built-up section.

From first principles

Derive transverse shear flow by balancing a longitudinal strip

Consider a slender prismatic beam undergoing elastic bending with moment \(M(x)\) and shear \(V=dM/dx\). Select a horizontal cut through the cross-section and keep the cap of material above it. Let \(y\) be measured from the neutral axis. The longitudinal bending stress \(-My/I\) has a resultant on the end of this cap proportional to its first area moment \(Q=\int_{\rm cap}y\,dA\):

\[N_{\rm cap}(x)=-M(x)Q/I .\]

Over distance \(dx\) the two end resultants differ. Longitudinal traction along the cut must balance this difference. Dividing its force by the beam length gives interfacial shear flow \(q_s\); spreading the flow over the local contact breadth \(b\) gives the elementary shear stress:

\[|q_s|=\left|dN_{\rm cap}/dx\right|=|VQ/I|,\]
\[|\tau|=|q_s|/b .\]

This is also the longitudinal load per length to be transferred by continuous glue or by discrete fasteners in a built-up beam. At a free outer surface the cap has zero area and flow vanishes. A small breadth at fixed \(Q\) can produce high local stress even when the section-average \(V/A\) is modest.

Background · Shear flow
\[q=VQ/I\]

In elementary beam theory the first moment Q belongs to the cut portion of the section. Shear flow is force per length. Divide by the local width to obtain shear stress.

Derivation and applications

Recover the entire shear resultant from stresses across a rectangular section

The average value \(V/A\) from force balance cannot locate the most heavily sheared fibres. Consider a slender rectangular beam of breadth \(b\) and depth \(h\), made from a homogeneous linear elastic material. Use a coordinate \(y\) upward from the neutral axis. At a section where bending moment is \(M(x)\), a strip carries longitudinal normal stress \(-My/I\). Changes in that stress along \(x\) must be balanced by longitudinal shear on the strip boundary. Take the entire cap of material above a horizontal cut at \(y\).

The resultant longitudinal force in this cap depends on its first area moment \(Q\):

\[N_{\rm cap}=-\frac{M}{I}Q(y),\]
\[Q(y)=\int_y^{h/2}y'b\,dy'=\frac b2\left(\frac{h^2}{4}-y^2\right).\]

Across length \(dx\) the cap force changes as the bending moment changes. With \(dM/dx=V\), equilibrium requires a shear flow of magnitude \(|VQ/I|\) per unit length. Dividing by the local breadth converts flow to shear stress:

\[\tau(y)=\frac{VQ(y)}{Ib},\]
\[I=bh^3/12 .\]

This expression uses the conventional signed direction for positive section shear. Substituting the rectangular first and second moments produces

\[\tau(y)=\frac{3V}{2bh}\left(1-\frac{4y^2}{h^2}\right),\]
\[\int_{-h/2}^{h/2}\tau(y)b\,dy=V .\]

The integral provides a direct equilibrium check, and the profile vanishes at the free surfaces. Maximum shear occurs at the neutral axis, precisely where bending normal stress is zero. The elementary profile is a beam-theory approximation away from concentrated loads or end details.

Worked example

Determine the force carried by the middle half-depth

Take \(b=30.0\) mm, \(h=60.0\) mm and \(V=4.80\) kN. The average is \(2.67\) MPa, but the centre reaches \(4.00\) MPa. Integrating the parabola only from \(-h/4\) to \(h/4\) gives the fraction \(11/16=0.6875\). The central half of the material therefore transmits \(3.30\) kN of the shear resultant; the outer two quarters together carry the remaining \(1.50\) kN.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Set a bond-line force requirement away from the neutral axis

Suppose the same beam is made from bonded strips and a glue line lies at \(y=h/4\). The local shear stress is \((3/4)(4.00)=3.00\) MPa and the flow across the \(30.0\) mm breadth is \(90.0\) kN/m. A \(20.0\) mm length of bond must transfer \(1.80\) kN under nearly constant local \(V\). Treating the bond stress as the section average would give \(1.60\) kN and miss the location dependence.

Why bending generates an interface force

Stack two boards and support them as a beam. Under a transverse load their neighbouring surfaces tend to slide. A bond or row of fasteners connects the boards so they can act as one deeper section. To calculate the demand on that connection, isolate only the material above the interface over a short beam length. Even when the external load points vertically, horizontal equilibrium of this isolated piece requires longitudinal shear at its lower face.

Under elastic bending about a centroidal principal axis, longitudinal stress is \(\sigma_x=My/I\) up to the chosen sign. Its resultant on the isolated partial area \(A'\) has magnitude \(N=M Q/I\), where \(Q=\int_{A'} y\,dA\) is its first moment about the neutral axis of the complete section. For constant cross-section, the difference between adjacent resultants is \(dN=(Q/I)\,dM\). The balancing interface force is \(q\,dx\).

\[q=\frac{dN}{dx}=\frac{VQ}{I},\]
\[\tau=\frac{q}{b_i}.\]

Here \(b_i\) is the local contacting width, and \(dM/dx=V\) for the sign convention used. Shear flow has units N/mm and stress has units N/mm². Evaluate \(Q\) with the portion on one side of the cut and \(I\) with the complete section. Using the second moment of an individual board defeats the assumption that the connection makes them share a curvature.

Select fastener spacing for an assembled beam

Two boards each 60 mm wide and 40 mm deep form an 80 mm deep rectangular beam. At a section carrying \(V=2.4\,\mathrm{kN}\), the complete centroidal second moment is

\[60(80)^3/12=2.56\times10^6\,\mathrm{mm^4}.\]

The upper board has area 2400 mm² and its centroid lies 20 mm above the interface, which coincides with the full neutral axis. Hence \(Q=48000\,\mathrm{mm^3}\).

The required flow is

\[2400(48000)/(2.56\times10^6)=45\,\mathrm{N/mm}.\]

An adhesive over the full contact width would carry an average longitudinal shear stress of \(45/60=0.75\,\mathrm{MPa}\). A single row of connectors rated for 900 N each in the idealised shear mode would require a spacing no greater than \(900/45=20\,\mathrm{mm}\). For a load varying significantly over a connector spacing, integrate the flow over its tributary length instead.

Recover the shear profile in a rectangle

At any elevation \(y\) in that rectangle the partial area extends from \(y\) to \(h/2\). Its first moment is

\[Q=b[(h/2)^2-y^2]/2.\]

Substituting \(I=bh^3/12\) gives

\[\tau(y)=[3V/(2bh)](1-4y^2/h^2).\]

The stress vanishes at the top and bottom free surfaces, reaching one and a half times the section-average transverse shear at mid-depth. The elastic bending stress, in contrast, vanishes at that mid-depth. A joint placed near the neutral axis can therefore carry substantial shear even when its local normal bending stress is small.

Where should fasteners become more closely spaced?

Under a uniform transverse load a simply supported beam has largest shear magnitude near the supports and zero shear at its symmetric midspan. With the same cross-section along the span, the connector force per unit length follows that shear diagram. Midspan may govern bending strength while the regions near the supports govern connector spacing.

Locate a transverse load that produces bending without twist

For a cross-section with one axis of symmetry, the shear centre lies somewhere on that axis. It can fall outside the section itself. To see why, consider a thin open channel with horizontal flanges of median length \(b\), a vertical web of median height \(h\) and uniform wall thickness \(t\). Both flanges extend to the right of the web. Apply a vertical shear \(V\); seek the transverse location of its line of action for zero torsional moment. Assume an undeformed cross-section and neglect terms of order \(t/h\) and \(t/b\).

Measure \(s\) from each free flange edge towards the web. The partial flange area for the longitudinal shear-flow formula is \(ts\), located about \(h/2\) from the horizontal neutral axis. Thus \(q_f(s)=Vtsh/(2I)\) in magnitude. Integrating along one flange gives horizontal resultant \(F_f=Vthb^2/(4I)\). Upper and lower flange forces point in opposite directions and form a couple of magnitude \(F_f h\). The web shear acts along the vertical web median and contributes no moment about that line. The vertical resultant must therefore be offset from the web by

\[e=\frac{F_f h}{V}=\frac{tb^2h^2}{4I},\]
\[I\simeq\frac{th^3}{12}+2bt(h/2)^2 .\]

Following the directions of the shear flows places the shear centre on the side of the web opposite the flanges. In a symmetric I-section, left and right flange couples cancel and the shear centre instead lies on the intersecting symmetry axes.

Compare centroidal and twist-free load paths

A thin channel has \(h=80\) mm, \(b=30\) mm and \(t=2.0\) mm. Its horizontal-axis second moment is

\[2(80)^3/12+2(30)(2)(40)^2=277333\,\mathrm{mm^4}.\]

The shear centre offset is \(2(30)^2(80)^2/[4(277333)]=10.38\,\mathrm{mm}\) to the left of the web. A 1.50 kN vertical force through the web therefore imposes a torque of magnitude \(1500(10.38)=15570\,\mathrm{N\,mm}=15.6\,\mathrm{N\,m}\) as well as bending. That additional torque can matter because a thin open section is relatively compliant in torsion.

The material centroid lies on the flange side at \(x_c=2(bt)(b/2)/(th+2bt)=6.43\,\mathrm{mm}\) to the right of the web. Loading through it increases the lever arm relative to the shear centre to 16.81 mm, giving 25.2 N m of torque. A bracket that transfers the load to the left of the web instead can reduce twist without enlarging the section. For a real channel with finite corner radii or unequal flange thicknesses, use its measured section geometry. The result also describes a free member; bracing against twist can replace a visible rotation by torsional reactions at the restraints.

One way to locate the shear centre experimentally is to apply the same small transverse load at several lateral offsets and measure twist per unit length in a region free of local load introduction. Linear elastic torsion predicts an approximately linear dependence of twist on offset. Its zero crossing locates the twist-free load line without requiring the torsion constant beforehand. A fixture restraining warping near the gauges can distort this test.

Study the full derivation

Why an open channel twists under a transverse load · Stress, deformation and structural measurement

A channel can twist even when a force passes through its centroid. Trace the longitudinal shear flow around its flanges, find the load line that produces bending without torsion, and design a loading experiment that separates the stiffness in bending from eccentricity relative to this shear centre.

Problem sheet 4 · Torsion and transferred shear

Trace torque and longitudinal shear through solid, tubular and assembled sections.

A hollow drive shaft

A 1.2 m long hollow shaft has outer and inner diameters 40 and 30 mm and carries 160 N m. Use \(G=78\,\mathrm{GPa}\). Find the peak shear stress and twist. If its length doubles, which result changes?

Reveal the worked solution

The polar moment is

\[\pi(0.040^4-0.030^4)/32=1.718\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm{m^4}.\]

The peak shear is \(160(0.020)/J=18.6\,\mathrm{MPa}\) and twist is

\[160(1.2)/(78\times10^9J)=0.0143\,\mathrm{rad}=0.821^\circ.\]

Doubling length doubles accumulated twist at unchanged torque, but the stress at each section is unchanged.

A box-section seam

A single-cell tube with median dimensions 100 by 60 mm and thickness 1.5 mm transmits 420 N m. Find its circulating shear flow, wall shear and force per fastener for one longitudinal seam at 25 mm pitch. What changes if thickness doubles without changing the median dimensions?

Reveal the worked solution

Using N and mm gives

\[q=420000/(2\times100\times60)=35\,\mathrm{N/mm}.\]

The wall stress is \(35/1.5=23.3\,\mathrm{MPa}\), and one fastener transmits \(35(25)=875\,\mathrm N\). Doubling thickness halves wall stress but leaves the equilibrium shear flow and nominal fastener force unchanged.

Connect a cap plate

A built-up beam carries 8 kN shear. Its second moment is \(12.0\times10^6\,\mathrm{mm^4}\). The cap plate area is 800 mm² and its centroid is 60 mm above the neutral axis. Two equivalent rows of fasteners share longitudinal shear. Find the force per fastener at 40 mm pitch. Determine the largest pitch for a 1.0 kN per-fastener limit.

Reveal the worked solution

The cap first moment is \(800(60)=48000\,\mathrm{mm^3}\), and \(q=8000(48000)/(12\times10^6)=32\,\mathrm{N/mm}\). Each row takes 16 N/mm under the equal-sharing assumption. At the specified pitch that is 640 N per fastener. The theoretical limiting pitch is \(1000/16=62.5\,\mathrm{mm}\); the practical arrangement must also supply the assumed composite action.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Torsional stiffness of shafts and closed sections; Transverse shear and connections in built-up beams. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 9 · Compatibility and redundant supports

Use elastic work to determine a support reaction that cannot be obtained from force and moment balance alone.

From first principles

Derive a redundant reaction by releasing one compatibility condition

In a statically indeterminate beam, force and moment balances leave more unknown reactions than independent equations. Choose one support force \(R\) as the redundant, release that support and confirm that the remaining structure is stable. Use positive displacement in the direction of \(R\). Under small linear elastic bending, the released structure moves at the former support by \(\delta_L\) under prescribed loads and by \(fR\) under the redundant. Restoring a support whose measured movement is \(\delta_s\) gives

\[\delta_L+fR=\delta_s,\]
\[R=(\delta_s-\delta_L)/f .\]

To calculate the flexibility instead of measuring it, apply a unit virtual force at the released coordinate. Its bending-moment diagram is \(m(x)\). The actual load-only diagram \(M_L\) produces curvature \(M_L/(EI)\). Equal virtual external and internal work then gives

\[\delta_L=\int \frac{mM_L}{EI}dx,\]
\[f=\int\frac{m^2}{EI}dx .\]

Positive rigidity makes \(f>0\) for an effective released coordinate. A negative computed reaction is meaningful for a bilateral support, but a compression-only contact requires checking whether the assumed support remains engaged.

Derivation and applications

How the stiffness of a prop sets a redundant reaction

Replacing a rigid roller by a calibrated spring provides a continuous path between a cantilever and a propped cantilever. Take a prismatic beam of length \(L\) clamped at its left end, a uniform downward load \(q\), and an initially just-touching vertical spring of stiffness \(k\) at its right tip. Choose downward deflection positive and denote the upward spring reaction by \(R\). Assume small bending, constant \(EI\), negligible shear deformation and no lift-off. Equilibrium alone cannot give the reaction because the clamp can transmit both force and moment.

Remove the spring temporarily. The tip deflection from the distributed load follows by integrating the free-beam moments or by virtual work with a unit downward tip force. The corresponding flexibility for an additional concentrated tip load is obtained using the same unit moment field:

\[w_q=\int_0^L\frac{[q(L-x)^2/2](L-x)}{EI}\,dx=\frac{qL^4}{8EI},\]
\[f=\int_0^L\frac{(L-x)^2}{EI}\,dx=\frac{L^3}{3EI}.\]

Restore the upward reaction. Superposition gives a smaller downward deflection \(w=w_q-fR\). At the same time the compressed spring carries \(R=kw\). These two descriptions must agree:

\[R=k(w_q-fR),\]
\[w=\frac{w_q}{1+kf}.\]

Introducing the dimensionless stiffness ratio \(\eta=kf\) makes both limiting support conditions transparent:

\[R=\frac{3qL}{8}\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}.\]

The free end is recovered as \(k\to0\). An infinitely stiff prop carries \(3qL/8\); even then the clamp still carries the rest of the distributed load. If a downward support settlement exceeds the free deflection, a compression-only spring loses contact and the bilateral force equation no longer applies.

Worked example

Predict the reaction at finite stiffness

Take \(L=2.00\) m, \(EI=24.0\) kN m², \(q=1.20\) kN/m and \(k=9.00\) kN/m. The free tip drops \(0.100\) m, while \(f=0.111\) m/kN and \(\eta=1.00\). The spring cuts the drop to \(0.0500\) m and supplies \(0.450\) kN upward. The clamp balances the remaining \(1.95\) kN vertical force.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Select a spring for a reaction target

The rigid-support limit for the same loading is \(0.900\) kN. To achieve \(80.0\%\) of that, solve \(\eta/(1+\eta)=0.800\) to obtain \(\eta=4.00\) and \(k=36.0\) kN/m. The predicted spring compression is

\[0.720/36.0=0.0200\,\mathrm{m}.\]

This agrees with \(w_q/(1+\eta)\) and distinguishes reaction fraction from displacement fraction.

Open this live calculation

Choose a redundant force

A cantilever with a vertical prop at its far end has one more reaction than the independent planar equilibrium equations can determine. A change of support elevation changes those reactions even though the applied loads stay the same. This sensitivity provides the additional equation: the displacement at the prop equals its specified settlement. Remove the prop temporarily and replace it by an unknown upward force \(R\). The released cantilever must be stable, and its displacement at that location must be calculable.

For small elastic deformation the effects of a known load and of the unknown force may be superposed. Write downward displacements as positive. Under a distributed downward load the released tip displaces by \(\delta_w\). An upward reaction displaces it by \(-fR\), where \(f\) is tip flexibility measured in metres per newton. If the prop settles downwards by \(s\), compatibility becomes

\[\delta_w-fR=s.\]

Find the coefficients by virtual work

For constant \(EI\), impose a unit downward load at the free tip. Its unit-load bending moment has magnitude \(m(x)=L-x\), measured from the fixed end. With a uniformly distributed load of intensity \(w\), the moment magnitude is \(M_w=w(L-x)^2/2\). Multiplying the actual and virtual moments and integrating gives

\[\delta_w=\int_0^L\frac{M_wm}{EI}\,dx=\frac{wL^4}{8EI},\]
\[f=\int_0^L\frac{m^2}{EI}\,dx=\frac{L^3}{3EI}.\]

For a rigid prop at its initial elevation \(s=0\), the reaction becomes

\[R=3wL/8.\]

The remaining vertical force at the fixed end is \(5wL/8\). The external support moment there has magnitude \(wL^2/2-RL=wL^2/8\). Recovering equilibrium at the end provides an independent check after using flexibility to close the calculation.

Predict the effect of a small support movement

Take \(L=3.0\,\mathrm m\), \(w=4.0\,\mathrm{kN/m}\) and \(EI=1.8\,\mathrm{MN\,m^2}\). Without the prop, \(\delta_w=0.0225\,\mathrm m\), and \(f=5.00\,\mathrm{mm/kN}\). Without settlement the prop takes 4.50 kN. With a measured downward settlement of 6 mm, it takes \((22.5-6)/5=3.30\,\mathrm{kN}\). The fixed-end vertical reaction rises to 8.70 kN and its moment magnitude rises from 4.50 to \(18.0-3.3(3)=8.10\,\mathrm{kN\,m}\).

A unilateral prop can push upwards but cannot pull the beam down. At settlements exceeding 22.5 mm the linear compatibility solution would give a negative prop reaction. In that case the beam hangs clear of the prop and behaves as the released cantilever. A bolted hold-down could sustain the negative reaction and would require a different contact model. Replacing the rigid prop by a spring gives another direct extension: its reaction moves its top by \(R/k\), so use \(\delta_w-fR=s+R/k\) and obtain \(R=(\delta_w-s)/(f+1/k)\). An increasingly compliant prop takes an increasingly small fraction of the load.

Check a reaction against a stiffness change

If all beam cross-sections gain twice their original bending stiffness, both the load-induced tip deflection and the tip flexibility halve. For a rigid prop with zero settlement their ratio stays constant, so reactions are unchanged. For a prescribed settlement, however, the term \(s/f\) doubles. A stiff structure can therefore develop large additional reactions from a small imposed support movement.

Use an interior reaction to join two spans

A rail carried over three supports provides a second use of the force method. Remove its central support to form a simple beam of total length \(2L\), retaining the central reaction \(R_B\) as an upward force to be found. Let both spans carry uniform downward load \(w\) with constant \(EI\). For downward displacement at the centre use a downward unit force there. On the left half its moment function is \(m=x/2\), while the corresponding uniform-load moment is

\[M=w x(2L-x)/2.\]

Symmetry provides the other half of the displacement integral.

\[\delta_w=\frac{2}{EI}\int_0^L M m\,dx=\frac{5wL^4}{24EI},\]
\[f_{BB}=\frac{2}{EI}\int_0^L (x/2)^2dx=\frac{L^3}{6EI}.\]

If the centre support is at its original elevation, setting \(\delta_w-R_B f_{BB}=0\) gives

\[R_B=5wL/4.\]

Each exterior reaction is then \(3wL/8\) from overall equilibrium. Taking moments at an internal section directly over the middle support gives

\[M_B=(3wL/8)L-wL^2/2=-wL^2/8.\]

Its negative sign describes hogging. An interior vertical support has not created a hinge in the beam; the continuous beam can transmit bending moment across that location even though the support itself supplies only a vertical force.

Set a settlement tolerance from a support load

For two 2.0 m spans under \(w=3.0\,\mathrm{kN/m}\) with \(EI=500\,\mathrm{kN\,m^2}\), the zero-settlement central reaction is

\[5(3)(2)/4=7.50\,\mathrm{kN}.\]

The two end reactions are 2.25 kN each and the middle moment is −1.50 kN m. The flexibility at the central support is

\[2^3/[6(500)]=0.002667\,\mathrm{m/kN}.\]

A 4.0 mm downward support settlement therefore reduces the central reaction by \(0.004/0.002667=1.50\,\mathrm{kN}\). Rebalancing the beam gives 3.00 kN at each end and zero moment over the middle support. The bar is still geometrically continuous; this particular combination of loading and support elevation happens to eliminate that moment.

If the central load cell must read at least 5.0 kN, setting \(R_B=7.50-s/f_{BB}\) gives a limiting downward settlement of 6.67 mm. Conversely, a 2 mm rise in the support increases its reading by 0.75 kN. The sign changes because the constraint prescribes a displacement and the reaction adapts. Loss of central contact would occur only at \(s=7.5(2.667)=20.0\,\mathrm{mm}\) in this one-sided arrangement. Temperature gradients that curve the rail create another imposed displacement in the same compatibility equation; measurements of elevation and temperature are needed to distinguish the two influences.

The numerical values illustrate a sign check for an after-installation alignment survey. At 4 mm descent the central reaction has fallen by 1.50 kN and each outer reaction must grow by 0.75 kN. Their three changes add to zero because the applied rail loading did not change. If all three load cells instead show smaller forces after settlement, some load has moved to another contact or one measurement reference has shifted. Compatibility selects the internal redistribution, but it never replaces the external balance of the complete rail.

A cross-level survey and a repeated load-cell tare can distinguish a genuine reaction change from a changed instrument zero.

Assemble a displacement model of a redundant braced joint

An entry in a truss stiffness matrix can be derived from the work of a single member. For a straight pin-ended bar with direction \(n=(c,s)\), length \(L\) and end translations \(u_i,u_j\), the first-order extension is \(n^T(u_j-u_i)\). Its force in tension is \(N=(EA/L)n^T(u_j-u_i)\), and its stored energy is \(EA[n^T(u_j-u_i)]^2/(2L)\). Differentiating with respect to the four translations gives the element stiffness \(K_e=(EA/L)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}nn^T&-nn^T\\-nn^T&nn^T\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). Each member adds its coefficients to the rows and columns of the actual end joints. Shared translations enforce compatibility. Summing member contributions at a joint enforces equilibrium. Prescribed displacements are imposed before solving the remaining equations.

Consider one moving joint connected to three fixed anchors. Give each bar axial stiffness \(k=10\,\mathrm{MN/m}\) and take the directions from each anchor to the moving joint as \((1,0),(0,1)\) and \((1,1)/\sqrt2\). The first two members supply independent horizontal and vertical stiffness. The diagonal adds \(k/2\) to every entry. After the anchors are constrained, the equation under load \(F=(4,0)\) kN becomes

\[k\begin{pmatrix}1.5&0.5\\0.5&1.5\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u_x\\u_y\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}4000\\0\end{pmatrix}.\]

Solving gives \(u_x=0.300\) mm, \(u_y=-0.100\) mm. The recovered forces \(k\,n^Tu\) are 3.00 kN tension, 1.00 kN compression and 1.414 kN tension. The diagonal projects 1.00 kN onto each axis, so the resisting components sum to 4.00 kN horizontally and zero vertically. As an energy check, \(\tfrac12 F^Tu=0.600\) J and \(\sum N_i^2/(2k)=0.600\) J. Two joint equilibrium equations alone cannot determine three member forces. If a brace softens, its contribution to \(K\) changes and the forces redistribute. This is the compatibility effect hidden by a purely static count.

Practise on sheet 5 · Find a mechanism before adding a diagonal spring. Further reading · MIT · 3.11 Mechanics of Materials · Trusses, “Matrix analysis of trusses”.

Trace a moving load onto an added prop

A cantilever of length \(L\) has a vertical prop at its free end. Under a point load at \(a\), the released tip displacement is \(P a^2(3L-a)/(6EI)\). Choose the prop force to cancel it using the tip flexibility \(L^3/(3EI)\).

Chapter 10 · From a strain rosette to principal stresses

Recover a complete plane-strain measurement at a surface from three gauges and infer the corresponding plane-stress state.

From first principles

Calculate the extension recorded by an angled strain gauge

A bonded straight gauge records fractional change of length along its own axis. Let adjacent material points be separated by \(d\mathbf x=\mathbf n\,ds\), where \(\mathbf n=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\) is a unit direction. For a smooth small displacement field \(\mathbf u\), their relative displacement is approximately \((\nabla\mathbf u)d\mathbf x\). To first order only the projection along the original separation changes its length:

\[\frac{\delta ds}{ds}=\mathbf n^\mathsf T(\nabla\mathbf u)\mathbf n=\mathbf n^\mathsf T\boldsymbol\epsilon\,\mathbf n,\]
\[\boldsymbol\epsilon=(\nabla\mathbf u+\nabla\mathbf u^\mathsf T)/2 .\]

The antisymmetric part represents infinitesimal rotation and has zero quadratic form. Writing engineering shear as \(\gamma_{xy}=2\epsilon_{xy}\) yields the observation equation

\[\epsilon_\theta=\epsilon_x\cos^2\theta+\epsilon_y\sin^2\theta+\gamma_{xy}\sin\theta\cos\theta .\]

Three independently oriented grids provide three scalar observations of a uniform in-plane strain tensor. For directions \(0,45,90\) degrees, their combination \(2\epsilon_{45}-\epsilon_0-\epsilon_{90}\) recovers engineering shear. Converting this strain to stress additionally requires elastic properties and a local plane-stress or plane-strain model.

Derivation and applications

Remove transverse gauge sensitivity before reconstructing strain

A resistance grid responds primarily to extension along its long axis. Its transverse sensitivity may still matter when a small shear strain is inferred from differences of readings. Model a gauge at angle \(\theta\) as reporting \(g_\theta=\epsilon_\theta+\xi\epsilon_{\theta+90^\circ}\) after longitudinal calibration, with known dimensionless transverse response \(\xi\). Assume uniform infinitesimal in-plane strain over a rectangular \(0^\circ,45^\circ,90^\circ\) rosette, identical temperature compensation and the same \(\xi\) for each grid. Readings and strains below are dimensionless.

At the two axial gauges the transverse direction of one is the longitudinal direction of the other. Sum and subtract their equations to separate mean extension from normal-strain difference:

\[g_0+g_{90}=(1+\xi)(\epsilon_x+\epsilon_y),\]
\[g_0-g_{90}=(1-\xi)(\epsilon_x-\epsilon_y).\]

At \(45^\circ\) and \(135^\circ\), small-strain transformation gives normal strain \((\epsilon_x+\epsilon_y)/2\pm\gamma_{xy}/2\), where \(\gamma_{xy}\) is engineering shear strain. Inserting both projections into the calibrated reading gives

\[2g_{45}=(1+\xi)(\epsilon_x+\epsilon_y)+(1-\xi)\gamma_{xy}.\]

Remove the trace using the first equation and recover the corrected shear and principal strains:

\[\gamma_{xy}=\frac{2g_{45}-g_0-g_{90}}{1-\xi},\]
\[\epsilon_{1,2}=\frac{\epsilon_x+\epsilon_y}{2}\pm\frac12\sqrt{(\epsilon_x-\epsilon_y)^2+\gamma_{xy}^2}.\]

If \(\xi=0\) this reduces to the ideal rosette. As \(\xi\) approaches unity all readings respond to the trace and directional information becomes poorly conditioned. A common temperature offset cancels from this shear combination but changes the inferred mean strain if no thermal correction is made.

Worked example

Recover a strain tensor from raw readings

For \(\xi=0.0200\) suppose \((g_0,g_{45},g_{90})=(298,200,-94)\) microstrain. The true strain sum is \(204/1.02=200\) microstrain, while its difference is \(392/0.98=400\) microstrain. Thus \(\epsilon_x=300\) and \(\epsilon_y=-100\) microstrain. The corrected engineering shear is \((400-298+94)/0.98=200\) microstrain. The principal values are \(100\pm223.6\), or \(323.6\) and \(-123.6\) microstrain.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Determine which calibration error matters for a pure shear test

Apply a reference pure engineering shear of \(500\) microstrain in the same axes. The corrected model predicts \(g_0=g_{90}=0\) and \(g_{45}=(1-0.0200)(500)/2=245\) microstrain. The ideal-gauge inversion would report \(490\) microstrain, low by \(2.0\%\). A uniform offset of \(12\) microstrain on every channel leaves this shear unchanged, separating the transverse response from the common offset in a calibration test.
