What a bonded gauge actually measures

A resistance gauge aligned with a line on a free surface records extension along that line. A single gauge cannot distinguish uniaxial tension from combined stretching and shear arranged to give the same directional extension. Bond three gauges at different angles near enough together that the local strain is approximately uniform. A rectangular rosette uses directions of zero, 45 and 90 degrees, all referred to the same positive surface axis.

Under infinitesimal deformation, a unit direction vector \(n=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\) experiences normal strain \(n^T\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}n\). Engineering shear strain \(\gamma_{xy}\) is twice the off-diagonal strain tensor component. Expanding the directional expression yields

\[\varepsilon_\theta=\varepsilon_x\cos^2\theta+\varepsilon_y\sin^2\theta+\gamma_{xy}\sin\theta\cos\theta .\]

The zero and 90° readings give \(\varepsilon_x,\varepsilon_y\) directly. The middle reading gives

\[\gamma_{xy}=2\varepsilon_{45}-\varepsilon_x-\varepsilon_y.\]

A gauge at minus 45° measures the opposite shear contribution. A sketch of the physical gauge labels avoids reversing the inferred shear sign during data reduction.

Introduce the local stress assumption

At an unloaded outer surface the normal and shear tractions on that surface vanish. With \(z\) normal to it, this gives local plane stress. For isotropic linear elasticity and mechanical strains obtained after temperature correction, invert the plane-stress compliance equations and use the shear modulus for the remaining component.

\[\sigma_x=\frac{E}{1-\nu^2}(\varepsilon_x+\nu\varepsilon_y),\]
\[\sigma_y=\frac{E}{1-\nu^2}(\varepsilon_y+\nu\varepsilon_x),\]
\[\tau_{xy}=\frac{E\gamma_{xy}}{2(1+\nu)} .\]

A strain rosette measures only the in-plane quantities. A nonzero through-thickness strain is consistent with plane stress because the in-plane stresses cause Poisson contraction. Beneath a contact patch the local traction condition changes, so the same stress recovery would need additional information.

Interpret the readings on a plate

Suppose a steel surface gives \(400,\ 250,\ -100\) microstrain at zero, 45 and 90 degrees. The recovered shear strain is \(500-400+100=200\) microstrain. With \(E=210\,\mathrm{GPa}\) and \(\nu=0.30\), the stresses are \(85.38,\ 4.62,\ 16.15\,\mathrm{MPa}\) for \(x,y,xy\), respectively. The shear is positive under the stated gauge orientation. Converting microstrain to \(10^{-6}\) before multiplying by MPa is essential.

The principal stresses are the eigenvalues of this symmetric matrix. Its mean normal stress is 45.0 MPa, and its Mohr-circle radius is

\[\sqrt{[(85.38-4.62)/2]^2+16.15^2}=43.50\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

Thus the two in-plane principal stresses are approximately 88.5 and 1.5 MPa. The major direction is \(\tfrac12\operatorname{atan2}(2\tau,\sigma_x-\sigma_y)=10.9^\circ\) from the zero gauge. A strain reading of −100 microstrain along \(y\) therefore does not imply compressive normal stress along that axis: Poisson contraction from the dominant \(x\) load accounts for much of that reading.

Resolve a uniform thermal signal

Uniform heating of an unconstrained isotropic plate adds the same free thermal strain \(\alpha\Delta T\) to each gauge reading, after allowing for the gauge instrumentation itself. It cancels from the inferred engineering shear because \(2-1-1=0\), but it persists in both recovered normal components. For a 20 K temperature rise and linear expansion \(12\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}\), subtract 240 microstrain from each reading before applying the mechanical plane-stress relation.

Reconstruct a surface strain from a delta rosette

The zero, 45 and 90 degree rosette is convenient but not the only useful placement. Introduce the strain-circle coordinates \(m=(\varepsilon_x+\varepsilon_y)/2,\ d=(\varepsilon_x-\varepsilon_y)/2,\ g=\gamma_{xy}/2\). Then each directional reading is \(e_\theta=m+d\cos 2\theta+g\sin2\theta\). Every installed gauge supplies one linear equation in these three unknowns. Gauge directions that are nearly parallel give nearly dependent equations, so a small error in an individual reading can then cause a large error in a recovered component.

For a delta rosette take directions \(0,60,120^\circ\). The doubled angles are equally spaced round the strain circle, so their cosine and sine values each sum to zero. Adding all three readings gives

\[m=(e_0+e_{60}+e_{120})/3.\]

The first reading then gives

\[d=e_0-m;\]

subtracting the last two cancels both \(m\) and their identical cosine terms and gives

\[g=(e_{60}-e_{120})/\sqrt3.\]

The in-plane principal strains and their physical orientation follow without a material assumption:

\[e_{1,2}=m\pm\sqrt{d^2+g^2},\]
\[\theta_1=\tfrac12\operatorname{atan2}(g,d).\]

The half-angle is necessary because a straight gauge has the same reading after a physical rotation of 180°. For an isotropic elastic solid the principal stress directions coincide with these strain directions. Orthotropic laminates can give stress and strain axes that differ, so converting a strain circle into stresses would require the material compliance in the specimen’s axes.

OneSimulation · How does a small gauge error become a large transverse strain?

A bonded gauge observes the normal strain along its axis. Reconstruct the two normal components and the engineering shear from three readings. Give only the middle reading a positive bias of 5 µε. The factor multiplying the shear in the observation matrix follows from rotating a symmetric tensor with \(\epsilon_{xy}=\gamma_{xy}/2\).

\[\epsilon_\theta=\epsilon_x\cos^2\theta+\epsilon_y\sin^2\theta+\gamma_{xy}\sin\theta\cos\theta.\]

OneSimulation solves the same three-unknown system for two different layouts. At 0°, 45° and 90°, the last gauge directly measures transverse strain. At 0°, 10° and 20°, all gauges see mostly the longitudinal direction. The reconstructed transverse strain now misses by −155.8 µε. Fitting three readings exactly supplies no independent check on accuracy.

"""Compare strain-rosette geometries with the same middle-gauge error."""
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.numerics.linear.matrices.dense_linear_algebra import solve_dense

true_strain = np.array([100., -60., 80.])  # ex, ey, engineering shear in µε
bias = 5.0  # µε added to the middle gauge
rotation = np.arange(0, 181)

def gauge_rows(degrees):
    angle = np.deg2rad(degrees)
    c, s = np.cos(angle), np.sin(angle)
    return np.column_stack((c**2, s**2, c*s))

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 4), constrained_layout=True)
for spacing, colour in [(45, "#19596a"), (10, "#a5442e")]:
    layout = gauge_rows([0, spacing, 2*spacing])
    readings = layout @ true_strain
    assert np.allclose(solve_dense(layout, readings), true_strain)
    readings[1] += bias
    inferred = solve_dense(layout, readings)
    error = gauge_rows(rotation) @ (inferred - true_strain)
    ax.plot(rotation, error, color=colour, label=f"Gauge spacing {spacing}°")
    print(f"{spacing}°: error at 90° = {error[90]:.2f} µε")

ax.axhline(0, color="0.5", linewidth=0.7)
ax.set(xlabel="Direction of predicted normal strain (degrees)",
       ylabel="Prediction error (µε)", xlim=(0, 180))
ax.legend()
fig.savefig("engineering-mechanics.png", dpi=170)

Download the Python example

Vary the parameters of this exact example in the chapter’s interactive calculation.

Check an installed rosette using an independent gauge

On a loaded aluminium plate a delta rosette reads \(e_0=350,\ e_{60}=200,\ e_{120}=-100\) microstrain. The mean is 150 microstrain; \(d=200\) and \(g=300/\sqrt3=173.2\) microstrain. Thus \(\varepsilon_x=350,\varepsilon_y=-50,\gamma_{xy}=346.4\) microstrain. The strain-circle radius is \(\sqrt{200^2+173.2^2}=264.6\) microstrain. Principal strains are 414.6 and −114.6 microstrain, with largest extension along \(\theta_1=20.45^\circ\) from the zero gauge.

Before converting these figures to stress, predict an independent reading at 90°. The directional equation gives \(m-d=-50\) microstrain, which can be checked with a fourth gauge. If it instead reads +50 microstrain, possible explanations include angular mislabelling, an adhesive defect or a spatial strain gradient across the footprint. In fact the maximum local slope of the directional reading with respect to angle is \(2\sqrt{d^2+g^2}\) per radian. A two-degree placement error can therefore contribute at most about \(529(2\pi/180)=18.5\) microstrain for this field to first order. An observed 100 microstrain discrepancy would be too large to explain by that tolerance alone. This cross-check assesses the measurement geometry before uncertainty in elastic moduli enters the interpretation.

Close to an equibiaxial strain state both \(d\) and \(g\) are small, so angle estimates from \(\operatorname{atan2}(g,d)\) can jump substantially between repeated readings. That sign of numerical sensitivity has a physical interpretation: when the strain circle collapses, every direction is principal. The nearly equal principal strain values may still be well determined even though a reported principal orientation is almost arbitrary. Quoting the circle radius together with the angle gives a reader enough information to decide whether the orientation is useful.

Problem sheet 5 · Redundants and measured strains

Combine displacement compatibility with equilibrium and distinguish strain measurements from stress components.

A cantilever with a spring prop

A released cantilever tip displacement under the specified distributed load is 14 mm downward; the tip flexibility is 4 mm/kN. It rests on an initially unloaded spring of stiffness 0.50 kN/mm. Find the spring reaction and tip displacement. Repeat for a rigid support initially 5 mm below the undeformed tip.

Reveal the worked solution

For the spring, \(14-4R=R/0.50\), hence \(R=2.333\,\mathrm{kN}\) and tip movement \(R/k=4.667\,\mathrm{mm}\). With the rigid gapped support, the freely deflected beam would reach the support, so \(14-4R=5\) and \(R=2.25\,\mathrm{kN}\). In both cases the positive reaction verifies compressive contact.

A point load on a propped beam

A cantilever of length \(L=2.4\,\mathrm m\) carries a downward point load \(P=6\,\mathrm{kN}\) at midspan and has a prop at the far tip, fixed at its original height. By virtual work, find the released tip displacement in terms of \(EI\), then determine prop reaction and the fixed-end moment magnitude.

Reveal the worked solution

Use actual moment \(P(a-x)\) for \(x<a=L/2\) and zero elsewhere. With tip unit-load moment \(L-x\), integrating gives

\[\delta=P a^2(3L-a)/(6EI)=5PL^3/(48EI).\]

The tip flexibility is \(L^3/(3EI)\), giving \(R=5P/16=1.875\,\mathrm{kN}\). The external fixed-end moment magnitude is

\[Pa-RL=7.2-4.5=2.70\,\mathrm{kN\,m}.\]

Identify a nearly pure shear state

An aluminium surface has readings \(+50,+400,-50\) microstrain at zero, 45 and 90°. Use \(E=70\,\mathrm{GPa}\), \(\nu=0.33\) and plane stress. Find engineering shear strain, shear stress and the principal stresses. Why can a large 45° reading occur with small strains along both coordinate axes?

Reveal the worked solution

The shear strain is \(2(400)-50-(-50)=800\) microstrain, giving \(\tau=70000(0.0008)/(2.66)=21.05\,\mathrm{MPa}\). The normal stresses are \(\pm2.63\,\mathrm{MPa}\), and the mean normal stress is zero. The principal stresses are

\[\pm\sqrt{2.63^2+21.05^2}=\pm21.22\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

Shear changes the diagonal lengths of a material square even when its side lengths change little.

Find a mechanism before adding a diagonal spring

A joint is first held only by horizontal bars with combined axial stiffness 12 MN/m. Can it resist a 1.5 kN load directed upwards in a linear truss model? Add a bar running up and to the right at 45° from its fixed anchor to the joint. With axial stiffness 6 MN/m, find both displacements and the diagonal force.

Show the worked solution

With only horizontal bars the free stiffness matrix is \(\operatorname{diag}(12,0)\) MN/m, so no finite solution exists for a nonzero vertical load. The added diagonal contributes 3 MN/m to all four entries, giving rows \((15,3)\) and \((3,3)\). In kN and mm the load vector is \((0,1.5)\). The first row gives

\[u_y=-5u_x.\]

The second then gives

\[u_x=-0.125,\ u_y=0.625\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Diagonal extension is \((0.625-0.125)/\sqrt2=0.3536\) mm and tension is 2.12 kN. The diagonal exerts 1.50 kN downwards and 1.50 kN leftwards on the joint. The horizontal bars exert 1.50 kN rightwards, so the joint balances the applied upward load.

Chapter 11 · Yield fronts and plastic bending capacity

Track the spread of yielding through a rectangular section and distinguish the first-yield moment from the fully plastic moment.

From first principles

Separate first yield from the plastic moment of a section

Take a homogeneous symmetric beam section in pure bending, with plane sections remaining plane and elastic-perfectly plastic material of yield stress \(\sigma_y\). Let \(c\) be the largest distance from the neutral axis. For small curvature \(\kappa\) longitudinal strain varies as \(\kappa y\), so the elastic stress distribution and bending moment are

\[\sigma_x=E\kappa y,\]
\[M=\int_A \sigma_x y\,dA=EI\kappa .\]

First yield occurs when the outermost stress magnitude reaches \(\sigma_y\). Continuing to bend can still raise the moment as deeper fibres reach yield. In the ideal fully plastic limit, the tensile and compressive blocks have stresses \(\pm\sigma_y\). Symmetry makes their axial forces cancel. The two distinct moment scales are therefore

\[M_y=\sigma_y I/c,\]
\[M_p=\sigma_y\int_A|y|\,dA .\]

For a rectangle of breadth \(b\) and total depth \(2c\), the moments become \(2b\sigma_y c^2/3\) and \(b\sigma_y c^2\). Their ratio \(3/2\) measures the stress redistribution available in the section. A complete frame mechanism generally needs several suitable plastic hinges, so section yield alone does not determine the collapse load.

Derivation and applications

Predict springback after partial plastic bending

A bent coupon can retain curvature after the applied moment is removed. Consider a homogeneous rectangular section of breadth \(b\), half-depth \(c\), Young’s modulus \(E\) and yield stress \(\sigma_y\). Assume an elastic-perfectly plastic material in monotone pure bending, plane sections and symmetric tension/compression response. Take curvature \(\kappa>0\) and magnitudes on the upper half. After yielding begins the elastic core extends from the centre to \(y_e=\sigma_y/(E\kappa)\); beyond it stress has saturated.

Integrate stress times lever arm separately in the core and yielded zones, then double by symmetry:

\[M=2b\!\left[\int_0^{y_e}E\kappa y^2\,dy+\int_{y_e}^{c}\sigma_y y\,dy\right]=b\sigma_y(c^2-y_e^2/3).\]

At first yield

\[\kappa_y=\sigma_y/(Ec);\]

the fully plastic limiting moment is

\[M_p=b\sigma_y c^2.\]

Eliminating \(y_e\) relates peak moment to peak curvature:

\[M/M_p=1-\tfrac13(\kappa_y/\kappa)^2,\qquad \kappa\ge\kappa_y .\]

Upon unloading, initially all fibres respond elastically. The removed moment corresponds to a change \(M/(EI)\) in curvature, with \(I=2bc^3/3\). Since \(M_p/(EI)=3\kappa_y/2\), the residual curvature is

\[\kappa_r=\kappa-\frac{M}{EI}=\kappa-\tfrac32\kappa_y\!\left[1-\tfrac13(\kappa_y/\kappa)^2\right].\]

At first yield the predicted residual is zero. The derivation assumes the unloading increment does not drive any fibre into reverse yield; that can be checked by its surface stress change. Real strain hardening or different moduli during unloading change the inferred springback.

Worked example

Calculate the unloaded radius of a bent strip

Take \(E=200\) GPa, \(\sigma_y=250\) MPa and thickness \(2c=5.00\) mm. First-yield curvature is \(0.500\) m⁻¹. Bend to \(\kappa=1.00\) m⁻¹ so only the middle half of the depth remains elastic. The peak moment is \((11/12)M_p\), unloading removes \(0.6875\) m⁻¹, and \(\kappa_r=0.3125\) m⁻¹. The strip springs back from a \(1.00\) m radius to a radius of \(3.20\) m.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Check for reverse yielding and residual surface stress

At that same peak, the tensile surface was at \(250\) MPa. Elastic unloading changes its stress by \(Ec(0.6875)=343.75\) MPa in the opposite direction, leaving \(-93.8\) MPa. This compressive residual remains below the \(250\) MPa reverse yield magnitude, consistently with elastic unloading. The unloaded part carries no applied moment even though its surface stress is nonzero; residual stresses elsewhere across the section balance its moment.

Open this live calculation

First yield occurs at a surface

A beam can retain moment capacity after its first surface fibres yield. Consider a straight rectangular bar of width \(b\), total depth \(2c\), equal tensile and compressive yield stresses \(\sigma_Y\), and an elastic, perfectly plastic law. Assume pure bending about its centroid, small strains and a compact section that avoids local buckling. Plane sections remain plane, so longitudinal strain varies as \(\kappa y\). Symmetry keeps the neutral axis at mid-depth for this material and section.

While the whole section is elastic, \(\sigma=E\kappa y\). As curvature increases, the first yield occurs when \(E\kappa_Y c=\sigma_Y\). Integrating stress times moment arm gives the elastic first-yield moment \(M_Y=EI\kappa_Y=\sigma_Y I/c\). A section with a narrow outer flange or unequal tensile and compressive material behaviour requires a fresh stress distribution and force equilibrium.

Integrate the elastic core and yielded layers

At a greater curvature let \(a=\sigma_Y/(E\kappa)\) be the half-depth of the remaining elastic core. Stress in the core grows linearly as \(\sigma_Y y/a\). Outside the core it remains at the yield magnitude. Each half of the section contributes the same bending moment. Splitting the integral at the moving yield front gives

\[M=2b\left[\int_0^a\frac{\sigma_Yy^2}{a}\,dy+\int_a^c\sigma_Yy\,dy\right]=b\sigma_Y(c^2-a^2/3).\]

At \(a=c\) this reproduces \(M_Y=(2/3)b\sigma_Yc^2\). As \(a\) approaches zero, \(M\) tends to \(M_p=b\sigma_Y c^2\), which is 1.5 times first yield for this solid rectangle. The yield fronts advance towards the centre while progressively larger curvature increments produce smaller changes of moment. The bending stiffness at first loading is therefore history dependent after yielding begins.

Quantify the onset and extent of plastic deformation

A 20 mm wide bar is 40 mm deep, with \(E=200\,\mathrm{GPa}\) and \(\sigma_Y=250\,\mathrm{MPa}\). Its first-yield curvature is \(250/(200000\times20)=6.25\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{mm^{-1}}\), or 0.0625 m⁻¹. First yield occurs at 1.333 kN m and the limiting plastic moment is 2.000 kN m. At twice the yield curvature the elastic core half-depth has fallen to 10 mm. Substitution gives

\[M=20(250)[400-100/3]=1.833\,\mathrm{kN\,m}.\]

Upon unloading, assume the stress changes elastically without reverse yield. The immediate moment–curvature slope is then \(EI\), so the recovered curvature is \(M/(EI)\), where \(I=20(40)^3/12=106667\,\mathrm{mm^4}\). It is \(8.59\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{mm^{-1}}\). The peak curvature was \(1.25\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{mm^{-1}}\), leaving about 0.0391 m⁻¹ residual curvature. A permanent bend can therefore coexist with zero applied bending moment. The residual internal stresses balance their own force and moment across the section.

Compare equal areas in different locations

Redistributing material towards the outer fibres changes the reserve between first yield and full plasticity. In the limiting idealisation of two very thin equal flanges of area \(A_f\) at \(y=\pm c\), essentially all material reaches yield together. The first-yield and plastic moments both approach \(2A_f\sigma_Y c\). A shape factor of 1.5 is specific to the solid rectangle and cannot be carried over to this ideal flange pair.

Build a collapse mechanism from plastic hinges

A section that reaches its plastic moment can undergo substantial further rotation in an ideal perfectly plastic model. A structure may still support additional external load because other sections can take up incremental bending. Collapse in a rigid–plastic beam idealisation occurs when enough hinges form to allow a compatible mechanism. Use virtual work at collapse: the external forces do work through compatible virtual displacements, and each plastic hinge dissipates \(M_p\) times its relative rotation. Elastic strain energy changes are neglected in this limit calculation.

Take a uniform beam fixed at both ends under a concentrated downward force \(P\) at midspan. Assume equal plastic moments of magnitude \(M_p\) in sagging and hogging, compact sections and adequate rotation capacity. Consider hinges at the two clamps and at midspan, joined by two rigid half-spans. A small downward virtual centre movement \(\delta\) rotates each half through \(\theta=2\delta/L\). The clamp hinges each rotate by \(\theta\), while the centre hinge rotates through \(2\theta\) because its adjacent halves slope in opposite directions. Work equivalence gives

\[P\delta=M_p(\theta+2\theta+\theta)=4M_p(2\delta/L),\]
\[P_c=8M_p/L.\]

This kinematically admissible mechanism gives an upper bound on true plastic collapse load under these assumptions. Here a matching statically admissible diagram can be constructed: end moments are \(-M_p\), the centre moment is \(+M_p\), and constant shear on each half gives

\[P/2=4M_p/L.\]

The matching bounds identify the exact ideal collapse load for this geometry and loading.

Separate first yield, section capacity and structural collapse

A mild-steel rectangular strip 30 mm wide and 60 mm deep spans 1.20 m between fixed clamps. Take yield stress 240 MPa. The first-yield section moment is \(M_Y=\sigma_Ybh^2/6=4.32\,\mathrm{kN\,m}\), and the fully plastic moment is

\[M_p=\sigma_Ybh^2/4=6.48\,\mathrm{kN\,m}.\]

For a central point force, elastic symmetry and compatibility give clamp and centre moment magnitudes \(PL/8\). First yield thus occurs at \(8(4.32)/1.20=28.8\,\mathrm{kN}\) and the ideal mechanism load is

\[8(6.48)/1.20=43.2\,\mathrm{kN}.\]

The factor of 1.5 here comes from the rectangular section’s reserve as yielding spreads. The three critical sections have equal absolute moment in this symmetric loading.

Release the right clamp rotation and the mechanism changes. For the simpler limiting case with both ends pinned, only one interior plastic hinge is needed and the moment maximum is \(PL/4\). The same section would collapse at \(4(6.48)/1.20=21.6\,\mathrm{kN}\), half the fixed-ended result. The comparison explains why a connection described as fixed must actually develop bending moment and plastic rotation before its contribution is credited. A brittle welded detail, lateral instability or a shear failure can prevent the assumed redistribution. The calculation supplies a limit for the stated deformation mode, not a prediction of every possible failure mechanism.

The location of the mechanism can be checked in an overload test by measuring rotations near both supports and on either side of midspan. A large rotation at a single connection before substantial rotation elsewhere indicates that the assumed distribution of hinge resistance is not being realised.

Study the full derivation

Springback and residual stress after plastic bending · Stress, deformation and structural measurement

After a rectangular strip has been bent beyond first yield, removing its end couple does not return it to its original curvature. Work out the loading and unloading paths fibre by fibre. Then explain why an unloaded strip can contain a substantial stress field with zero net force and zero net bending moment.

Chapter 12 · Crack driving force and fracture assessment

Use an energy balance and a stress-intensity model to distinguish crack-controlled fracture from yielding of the gross section.

From first principles

Relate crack extension to the energy released by elastic compliance

Consider a slowly loaded linear elastic specimen of thickness \(B\), applied force \(P\), measured displacement \(\delta\) and crack coordinate \(a\). Write \(C(a)=\delta/P\). The projected area convention is \(dA=B\,da\). On a fixed crack the linear loading curve stores strain energy equal to the triangle below its force–displacement graph:

\[U=\tfrac12P\delta=\tfrac12P^2C(a).\]

For a dead force held constant while the crack extends, the external force performs additional work \(P\,d\delta\). Subtract the load potential \(P\delta\) from the stored energy and differentiate with respect to area to obtain the driving force for extension:

\[\Pi=U-P\delta=-\tfrac12P^2C,\]
\[G=-\left.\frac{d\Pi}{dA}\right|_{P}=\frac{P^2}{2B}\frac{dC}{da}.\]

The units are J/m². An increase of compliance can free energy to create new surfaces and to dissipate energy near the crack. A material resistance measured under matching conditions is needed to predict onset. In linear elastic mode I, the local intensity relation \(G=K_I^2/E'\) connects this global energy measure with the near-tip field.

Background · Stress intensity
\[K_I=Y\sigma\sqrt{\pi a}\]

Opening-mode intensity has units of stress times the square root of length. Geometry and the definition of crack length are part of the calibration.

Derivation and applications

How machine compliance changes the energy available to a crack

A tensile fixture can release stored elastic energy as a crack grows even when the grips do not move. To quantify this, treat a specimen of thickness \(B\) with crack coordinate \(a\) and measured opening compliance \(C(a)=\delta_s/P\). Use a single projected crack-area increment \(B\,da\) consistent with the stated convention. Let an elastic testing machine in series have constant compliance \(C_m\), and assume quasistatic linear elastic loading with negligible dissipative processes outside the crack tip. At fixed total grip opening \(\Delta\) the force must change as the specimen compliance changes:

\[\Delta=P(C+C_m),\]
\[U=\tfrac12P^2(C+C_m)=\frac{\Delta^2}{2(C+C_m)} .\]

No further external work enters at fixed \(\Delta\). Differentiate the stored energy with respect to projected area. Since \(C_m\) is independent of the crack coordinate, only the specimen derivative appears:

\[G=-\frac1B\left.\frac{dU}{da}\right|_\Delta=\frac{\Delta^2}{2B(C+C_m)^2}\frac{dC}{da}=\frac{P^2}{2B}C'(a).\]

At a given instantaneous force this has the familiar force-based value, but its variation during crack extension depends on compliance control. Take the idealized measured relation \(C=ca^3\) over a limited range. Logarithmic differentiation at fixed imposed opening gives

\[\frac{1}{G}\frac{dG}{da}=\frac2a-\frac{6C}{a(C+C_m)} .\]

For constant fracture resistance, a decreasing driving force after slight growth favours arrest. In this compliance model it occurs when \(C_m<2C\). This local comparison assumes a small crack extension within the calibrated range and a material whose fracture resistance is not changing appreciably.

Worked example

Estimate driving force from a coupon measurement

At \(a=40.0\) mm take \(C=2.00\) micrometres/N, \(C_m=1.00\) micrometre/N, \(B=10.0\) mm and \(\Delta=0.600\) mm. The force is \(200\) N, and the local cubic slope is \(C'=3C/a=1.50\times10^{-4}\) N⁻¹. Substitution yields \(G=300\) J/m². The calculated logarithmic slope is \(-50.0\) m⁻¹, predicting about a \(5\%\) decrease for an initial additional millimetre of extension.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Compare a compliant test frame at the same force

Increase machine compliance to \(6.00\) micrometres/N and impose \(\Delta=1.60\) mm so the starting force stays \(200\) N. The initial \(G\) remains \(300\) J/m², but the slope is now \(+12.5\) m⁻¹. A millimetre of additional crack length initially raises the driving force by about \(1.25\%\). The specimen itself has not changed; the frame now delivers a different load history as the crack extends.

A sharp crack changes the question

An uncracked tensile coupon can often be assessed by comparing nominal stress with a yield stress. A sharp pre-existing crack concentrates the stress into a small region near its tip. Within linear elastic fracture mechanics the leading opening-mode field has magnitude proportional to \(K_I/\sqrt{2\pi r}\) at distance \(r\) ahead of the tip. The unbounded mathematical stress signals the limits of the elastic tip description. A material toughness measured under comparable conditions instead sets a threshold for crack extension.

A common representation is \(K_I=Y\sigma\sqrt{\pi a}\), where \(a\) is the specified crack dimension, \(\sigma\) is a stated remote stress, and \(Y\) describes the geometry and loading. For a central crack in a sufficiently wide plate \(a\) is the half-length and \(Y\) approaches one. For a short edge crack in a wide plate \(a\) is its depth and a common approximation is \(Y\approx1.12\). Finite width, bending or a different crack shape changes the factor.

Relate compliance to an energy release rate

In a linear test specimen let load \(P\), opening displacement \(u\), and compliance \(C(a)=u/P\) refer to the same load point. At fixed load the stored elastic energy is \(P^2C/2\). The external load does work \(P\,du=P^2\,dC\) as the crack extends. The released potential energy is the difference, \(P^2 dC/2\). For a through crack with one advancing front and specimen thickness \(B\), its projected area increases by \(B\,da\), giving

\[G=\frac{P^2}{2B}\frac{dC}{da}=\frac{K_I^2}{E'},\]
\[E'=E\ {\rm (plane\ stress)},\]
\[E'=E/(1-\nu^2)\ {\rm (plane\ strain)}.\]

The compliance argument explains how a distant load–displacement measurement can identify energy available at a crack. Define the crack area consistently: a symmetric specimen in which two tips advance together changes projected area twice as quickly for the same increment of half-length.

Infer a critical flaw size

Take a wide plate with an edge crack of depth 3.0 mm, tensile remote stress 160 MPa and illustrative applicable toughness \(K_c=35\,\mathrm{MPa\sqrt m}\). The estimate \(K_I=1.12(160)\sqrt{\pi(0.003)}=17.4\,\mathrm{MPa\sqrt m}\) uses metres for crack depth. Holding geometry factor and stress fixed, the critical depth is \(a_c=[35/(1.12\times160)]^2/\pi=0.0121\,\mathrm m\), about 12.1 mm.

At twice the stress this depth falls by a factor of four, to approximately 3.0 mm. Before using that extrapolation, check remaining net section, applicability of the wide-plate geometry and whether plasticity near the crack is small compared with crack and ligament dimensions. Toughness depends on temperature, constraint and loading rate. An inspection interval would additionally require a crack-growth law, a load history and a detection limit. The calculated critical depth describes one load state and cannot by itself predict the number of cycles remaining.

Interpret a crack-length uncertainty

At fixed remote stress and geometry factor a small relative error in crack depth gives approximately half that relative error in stress intensity, because \(dK/K=da/(2a)\). A 0.3 mm uncertainty on a 3 mm measurement therefore changes this estimated intensity by about five percent.

Integrate stable fatigue growth up to inspection

Fracture toughness characterises an instability at a given load. Damage tolerance also asks how long a detected crack takes to reach an inspection or retirement size under repeated loading. For illustration, suppose tests for the service material and stress ratio support a Paris relation \(da/dN=C(\Delta K)^m\) in its intermediate stable growth range. Use crack depth \(a\) in metres and intensities in MPa√m. The coefficient \(C\) must correspond to those units, stress ratio and environment. This empirical law generally changes near threshold and near final instability.

For constant stress range \(\Delta\sigma\), fixed geometry factor \(Y\) and exponent \(m=3\), substitute \(\Delta K=Y\Delta\sigma\sqrt{\pi a}\). Crack advance per cycle then scales as \(a^{3/2}\); the same stress history grows a larger crack faster. Separate variables between measured depth \(a_i\) and removal depth \(a_f\). Integrating yields

\[N=\frac{2(a_i^{-1/2}-a_f^{-1/2})}{C(Y\Delta\sigma)^3\pi^{3/2}} .\]

This prediction decreases to zero as the two depths approach each other. It scales with the inverse cube of stress range at fixed bounds. If the geometry factor changes appreciably with crack depth, retain \(Y(a)\) inside the integral. The maximum stress in the cycle is needed separately to compare \(K_{\max}\) with applicable toughness, because it is the range that drives the adopted fatigue law.

Compare two inspection thresholds on the same plate

Consider a wide plate with \(Y=1.12\), \(\Delta\sigma=80\,\mathrm{MPa}\), \(\sigma_{\max}=140\,\mathrm{MPa}\), \(m=3\), and illustrative \(C=2.0\times10^{-12}\,(\mathrm{m/cycle})/(\mathrm{MPa}\sqrt{\mathrm m})^3\). Suppose the measured growth-law range includes depths between 2 and 8 mm for this loading. At 2 mm,

\[\Delta K=1.12(80)\sqrt{\pi(0.002)}=7.10\,\mathrm{MPa\sqrt m};\]

at 8 mm it doubles to 14.2. The growth rate at the upper limit is consequently eight times the lower-limit rate.

The integral denominator is \(2.0\times10^{-12}(89.6)^3\pi^{3/2}=8.01\times10^{-6}\) in the stated units, and the numerator is

\[2(22.36-11.18)=22.36\,\mathrm{m^{-1/2}}.\]

The estimated interval is \(2.79\times10^6\) cycles. If the smallest reliably detected crack were 4 mm, the available interval to 8 mm would fall to \(2(15.81-11.18)/(8.01\times10^{-6})=1.16\times10^6\) cycles. Improving early detection has a measurable value in this model. At retirement depth \(K_{\max}=24.9\,\mathrm{MPa\sqrt m}\); compare that independently with the applicable toughness. Uncertainty in detection, loading, environment and growth coefficients would enter an actual inspection decision rather than treating this calculated mean growth interval as a guaranteed service interval.

A useful sensitivity check is to increase the entire stress range by ten percent while keeping the inspection depths and coefficient fixed. The Paris estimate falls by \(1/1.1^3=0.751\), reducing the 2.79 million-cycle interval to about 2.10 million cycles. If the maximum stress also rises by ten percent, retirement intensity increases by the same ten percent to 27.4 MPa√m. Those two changes affect different checks and cannot be combined into a single linear safety multiplier. A record of both stress range and stress maximum is therefore more informative than a peak-only reading for planning inspections.

Both quantities can be recovered from the two measured stress extremes if the counting convention and gauge reference are retained.

Which flaw sizes make fracture the controlling limit?

Consider a wide plate with a small edge crack, geometry factor \(Y=1.12\) and conditional toughness \(K_c=35\,\mathrm{MPa}\sqrt{\mathrm m}\). Rearrange \(K=Y\sigma\sqrt{\pi a}\) and compare with a 320 MPa yield scale. Convert the crack length to metres.

Problem sheet 6 · Plastic deformation and crack driving force

Check the limits of an elastic calculation using yielding, residual deformation and fracture energy.

How much of the cross-section has yielded?

A rectangular strip 12 mm wide and 30 mm deep has elastic–perfectly plastic yield stress 200 MPa. Determine the first-yield and plastic moments. At a curvature three times the yield curvature, find the depth of the elastic core and resulting moment.

Reveal the worked solution

With \(c=15\,\mathrm{mm}\),

\[M_p=b\sigma_Yc^2=540\,\mathrm{N\,m},\]
\[M_Y=2M_p/3=360\,\mathrm{N\,m}.\]

The elastic half-depth is \(a=c/3=5\,\mathrm{mm}\), giving a 10 mm deep core. The moment is

\[12(200)(15^2-5^2/3)=520\,\mathrm{N\,m}.\]

Yield has spread far inward for a comparatively small increase over the first-yield moment.

Changing the inspection threshold

A wide plate with a central through crack has applicable toughness 45 MPa√m at the service temperature. With remote tension 180 MPa and \(Y=1\), calculate critical half-length and full length. If inspection finds a full crack length of 16 mm, what remote stress gives the critical intensity?

Reveal the worked solution

The critical half-length is \(a_c=(45/180)^2/\pi=0.0199\,\mathrm m\), or 19.9 mm; the full length is 39.8 mm. The measured half-length is 8 mm, so

\[\sigma_c=45/\sqrt{\pi(0.008)}=284\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

Both predictions assume small-scale yielding and adequate plate width. Using full length inside the square root would underpredict the critical stress.

Infer release rate from two readings

A specimen with one advancing crack front has thickness 10 mm. Its measured compliance increases from 0.0250 to 0.0270 mm/kN as crack length rises by 1.0 mm. Under a fixed load of 4 kN estimate release rate. For plane stress with \(E=70\,\mathrm{GPa}\), find the corresponding intensity.

Reveal the worked solution

The slope is

\[0.0020\,\mathrm{kN^{-1}}=2.0\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{N^{-1}}.\]

Thus

\[G=4000^2(2.0\times10^{-6})/[2(0.010)]=1600\,\mathrm{J/m^2}.\]

Then

\[K=\sqrt{EG}=10.58\,\mathrm{MPa\sqrt m}.\]

This uses a local finite-difference slope, so significant curvature of the compliance data would call for measurements at closer crack lengths.

Separate load capacity from serviceability

A simply supported steel beam of span 2.00 m carries a 2.00 kN centre load. Its solid rectangular section is 40.0 mm wide by 80.0 mm deep. Take E = 200 GPa, allowable bending stress 120 MPa and a service deflection limit L/400. Find the admissible centre load. Would rotating the section by 90° change which limit governs?

Worked solution

Symmetry gives reactions P/2 and the peak bending moment PL/4. With the 80 mm side vertical the second moment of area is bh³/12 = 1.707 × 10⁻⁶ m⁴ and the elastic section modulus is I/(h/2) = 4.267 × 10⁻⁵ m³. Under the specified load the centre moment is 1000 N m and the extreme-fibre stress is 23.4 MPa.

Integrating the elastic curvature with zero displacement at the supports and zero slope at midspan gives δ = PL³/(48EI). At 2.00 kN this is 0.977 mm. The stress limit gives 10.24 kN and the deflection limit of 5.00 mm also gives 10.24 kN. Their coincidence is particular to these dimensions and limits.

On rotating the beam, the new second moment is one quarter of the original and the section modulus one half. The stress limit now allows 5.12 kN, while deflection allows only 2.56 kN. The material, mass and support reactions stayed the same. The calculation assumes elastic bending and effective lateral restraint; connection and local bearing capacities require separate data.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Yield fronts and plastic bending capacity; Crack driving force and fracture assessment. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 13 · From cut forces to local equilibrium

A support reaction tells you the total force entering a part. To predict what an inclined internal surface carries, keep track of both the orientation of the cut and the direction of the force across it.

A force that depends on the surface you choose

Imagine a small triangular wedge cut out of a thin, slowly loaded plate. Its sloping face has unit outward normal \(\mathbf n=(n_x,n_y)\) and area \(A\). The two faces normal to the coordinate axes have areas \(A n_x\) and \(A n_y\) when both components are positive. Name the internal force per unit area on a face its traction. Opposite faces of the same cut carry equal and opposite tractions. Gravity and acceleration act on volume, which shrinks faster than area as the wedge shrinks. Dividing its force balance by \(A\) therefore gives a relation that no longer contains its size.

\[\mathbf t(\mathbf n)=n_x\mathbf t(\mathbf e_x)+n_y\mathbf t(\mathbf e_y)=\boldsymbol\sigma\mathbf n .\]

The columns of the stress matrix are the two coordinate-face tractions. Their linear combination supplies the force on any inclined face. A small square additionally needs zero net moment. To leading order, the shear forces on opposite faces form two couples of opposite sign and equal lever arm. Their balance requires \(\sigma_{xy}=\sigma_{yx}\) for an ordinary material without internal body couples.

Background · Traction
\[\mathbf t(\mathbf n)=\boldsymbol\sigma\mathbf n\]

Specify the plane normal before projecting the force per area into normal and shear parts. A stress component is not a net force until integrated over an area.

Why a gradient of stress carries a body force

Now use a tiny rectangular element of side lengths \(dx,dy\) and unit thickness. The right-face contribution to horizontal force is \(\sigma_{xx}(x+dx,y)dy\); the left face contributes its negative evaluated at \(x\). Their difference is \(\partial_x\sigma_{xx}\,dx\,dy\) to leading order. Resolve the upper and lower shear tractions in the same way. Add a prescribed horizontal force per volume \(b_x\) and divide by volume. Repeating in the vertical direction produces two independent equations.

\[\partial_x\sigma_{xx}+\partial_y\sigma_{xy}+b_x=0,\]
\[\partial_x\sigma_{xy}+\partial_y\sigma_{yy}+b_y=0 .\]

These equations say nothing yet about how much the material stretches. They can test an assumed stress field before the elastic modulus has even been measured. A candidate must also reproduce the applied boundary tractions. Satisfying the interior equations alone leaves many possible stresses.

Worked example · the grip on a diagonal bonding plane

At a measurement point let \(\sigma_{xx}=60,\sigma_{yy}=20,\sigma_{xy}=10\) MPa. The outward normal is \((0.6,0.8)\). Matrix multiplication gives \(\mathbf t=(44,22)\) MPa. Projection along the normal gives \(0.6(44)+0.8(22)=44.0\) MPa tension. Projection on the unit tangent \((-0.8,0.6)\) gives \(-22.0\) MPa. Reversing the tangent changes the sign of reported shear. Reversing the surface normal reverses the traction vector itself. For a uniform 100 mm² patch the force has components 4.40 and 2.20 kN. Using only normal stress would miss the tangential load that the adhesive must transmit.

Worked example · a specimen with a known stress gradient

Stand a bar upright, support it at its bottom and leave the top free. Use upward \(y\), length \(L\), constant density \(\rho\), and tension positive. The vertical equation reads \(d\sigma_{yy}/dy-\rho g=0\). Integrating from the free top gives

\[\sigma_{yy}=\rho g(y-L).\]

For a 3.0 m aluminium bar of density 2700 kg/m³ the base stress is −79.5 kPa. Its upward support traction is positive because the bottom face has downward outward normal. A uniform cross-sectional area of 200 mm² gives a base reaction of 15.9 N, equal to the weight. The local and global checks involve different quantities but must agree.

Find the angles where the shear traction passes through zero. Compare each with an extremum of the normal traction. Change the sign of the imposed xy shear, sketch the new angles before moving the slider, and check whether the two extreme normal stresses change.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · test a candidate before using a material law

In a region with \(x,y\) in metres, propose \(\sigma_{xx}=ax,\ \sigma_{xy}=-ay,\ \sigma_{yy}=0\), where \(a=2.0\) MPa/m. Does the field need a body force? Find the traction at \(x=0.30,\ y=0.20\) m on a face normal to positive \(x\).

Check the balance and the boundary reading

The horizontal divergence is \(a-a=0\). The vertical divergence is zero because the shear has no \(x\) variation. No body force is needed. The face sees the first column of the matrix, \((0.60,-0.40)\) MPa. A traction-free exterior at that location would reject this field even though it satisfies both interior equations.

Further reading · A. F. Bower, Applied Mechanics of Solids, §2.3, internal forces and stress

Chapter 14 · How a lateral restraint changes a measured modulus

A short compression test can report a larger stiffness than a tensile coupon cut from the same material. Derive the difference from the strains that the fixture permits.

Separate changes of volume and changes of shape

For small strain, uniaxial tension \(\sigma\) gives longitudinal strain \(\sigma/E\) and transverse strain \(-\nu\sigma/E\). These are experimental definitions of Young’s modulus and Poisson’s ratio for an isotropic material. In a linear solid the three normal coordinate loading cases can be superposed. Under tensile stresses positive, this gives

\[E\epsilon_x=\sigma_x-\nu(\sigma_y+\sigma_z),\]
\[E\epsilon_y=\sigma_y-\nu(\sigma_x+\sigma_z),\]
\[E\epsilon_z=\sigma_z-\nu(\sigma_x+\sigma_y).\]

Add the three equations. The sum of the small normal strains is the relative volume change because the product \((1+\epsilon_x)(1+\epsilon_y)(1+\epsilon_z)\) retains only first-order terms. Under compressive pressure \(p\) all three normal stresses are \(-p\). Hence

\[\frac{\Delta V}{V}=-\frac{3(1-2\nu)}{E}p=-\frac{p}{K},\]
\[K=\frac{E}{3(1-2\nu)} .\]

Large bulk stiffness can coexist with moderate shear stiffness. Near \(\nu=0.5\), changing volume becomes difficult, while changing shape can remain easy. This distinction matters when interpreting a pad compressed between smooth plates and the same pad enclosed within a rigid sleeve.

Derive the stiffness in a close-fitting sleeve

Apply axial stress \(\sigma_z\) to a specimen with both lateral strains constrained to zero. Assume uniform deformation away from the ends and identical conditions in \(x\) and \(y\). Symmetry gives

\[\sigma_x=\sigma_y=s.\]

The first strain law becomes \(0=(1-\nu)s-\nu\sigma_z\), so the sleeve must supply a lateral stress even though no lateral load was specified at the start.

\[s=\frac{\nu}{1-\nu}\sigma_z,\]
\[\frac{\sigma_z}{\epsilon_z}=\frac{E(1-\nu)}{(1+\nu)(1-2\nu)}=M .\]

The constraint modulus \(M\) increases rapidly near incompressibility. It is the ratio of a particular stress to a particular strain in this test. The material constants in the original three-dimensional law continue to apply in both fixtures. A supposedly free compression test with strong friction on the platens may fall between ideal free and fully confined behaviour.

Worked example · recover the sleeve reaction

A linearly elastic sample has \(E=30\) MPa and \(\nu=0.40\). Its thickness is 20 mm and the axial stress is −0.30 MPa. With free sidewalls the axial strain is −0.0100 and the contraction is 0.200 mm. Inside the ideal rigid sleeve, \(M=64.29\) MPa, giving contraction \(20(0.30/64.29)=0.0933\) mm. Each lateral stress is −0.200 MPa. Substitution into the original \(x\)-strain equation gives zero, a direct test of the force that was inferred from compatibility.

Worked example · infer a bulk property independently

Compress the same material hydrostatically by 0.15 MPa. The measured constants predict \(K=50.0\) MPa and a volume loss of 0.300%. A 40 cm³ specimen would lose 0.120 cm³. Using its sleeve modulus in this calculation would underestimate the volume change. In hydrostatic compression all three strains are equal. In the sleeve only one strain can be nonzero. A constitutive test must specify what is imposed on the other faces and what the measurement records.

Start with zero Poisson ratio. Explain why the two compression curves coincide. Raise the ratio to 0.45 and read the force required for equal movement in the two tests. Which part of the constitutive law causes the slopes to separate?

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · identify a Poisson effect from two tests

An axial test gives \(E=24\) MPa. A hydrostatic test gives \(K=40\) MPa. Infer the Poisson ratio and predict the axial stress required for 0.5% compression in an ideal sleeve. Which additional measurement would reveal slipping or a clearance in the sleeve?

Reconstruct the constraint

Solving \(E=3K(1-2\nu)\) gives \(\nu=0.40\). The constraint modulus is

\[24(0.60)/(1.40\times0.20)=51.43\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

For \(\epsilon_z=-0.005\) the stress is −0.257 MPa. The ideal lateral strain is zero everywhere in the uniform test section. A transverse displacement measurement along the midsection tests this condition directly. Near the ends the same reading can include fixture effects.

Further reading · A. F. Bower, Applied Mechanics of Solids, §3.2, linear elastic material behaviour

Chapter 15 · Radial displacement and hoop stress in a thick ring

As a soft sleeve presses outward on a ring, the inside and outside radii move by different amounts. A cut through the thickness reveals why replacing the entire wall by one hoop stress loses information.

Let one radius displace by a small amount

Take a long annular section or a thin annular disk loaded uniformly around its circumference. Here use the disk idealisation with free faces and plane stress. Let \(a,b\) be its inner and outer radii. Axial displacement effects near contact edges are excluded. If the material point at \(r\) moves outward by \(u(r)\), a radial line length \(dr\) changes by \(du\), while a circumference \(2\pi r\) changes by \(2\pi u\). Dividing each change by the original length gives

\[\epsilon_r=u'(r),\]
\[\epsilon_\theta=u/r,\]
\[\sigma_r=\frac{E}{1-\nu^2}\left(u'+\nu\frac{u}{r}\right),\]
\[\sigma_\theta=\frac{E}{1-\nu^2}\left(\frac{u}{r}+\nu u'\right).\]

On a small annular wedge the two radial faces have different areas. The two hoop tractions also have a net radial component, since their directions differ by the angle of the wedge. Resolving radial forces and dividing by its volume leaves \(d\sigma_r/dr+(\sigma_r-\sigma_\theta)/r=0\). Inserting the material equations eliminates both stresses.

\[u''+\frac{u'}{r}-\frac{u}{r^2}=0,\]
\[u=C_1 r+C_2/r .\]

To see where the two terms come from, try \(u=r^n\). All terms scale as \(r^{n-2}\); their coefficients add to \(n^2-1\). Both \(n=1\) and \(n=-1\) are admissible because the domain excludes \(r=0\). For a solid disk the singular term would have to vanish at its centre.

Use pressure as a radial traction

The radial and hoop stresses take the forms \(A-B/r^2\) and \(A+B/r^2\). Let compressive inner contact pressure be \(p\), and the outside be free. The normal stress conditions are \(\sigma_r(a)=-p\), \(\sigma_r(b)=0\). These two equations fix both constants.

\[A=\frac{pa^2}{b^2-a^2},\]
\[B=\frac{pa^2b^2}{b^2-a^2},\]
\[u(r)=\frac{(1-\nu)Ar+(1+\nu)B/r}{E}.\]

Stress depends on geometry and the applied tractions. Displacement additionally depends on stiffness. The outer surface can therefore carry tensile hoop stress while having zero radial traction. A strain gauge aligned around that surface responds to the hoop direction.

Worked example · compare inside and outside readings

Take \(a=20,\ b=40\) mm, \(p=3.0\) MPa, \(E=70{,}000\) MPa and \(\nu=0.30\). The constants are \(A=1.00\) MPa and \(B=1600\) MPa mm². Inner hoop stress is

\[1+1600/400=5.00\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

Outer hoop stress is 2.00 MPa. The outer radial displacement is

\[40(2/70000)=0.001143\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

At the inner wall \(\epsilon_\theta=(5-0.30(-3))/70000\); multiplying by 20 mm gives 0.001686 mm. A uniform dilation fitted to the outer reading would predict the wrong opening at the inner face.

Worked example · why a thin-wall balance still gets a resultant right

Cut the same ring along a diameter. The mean hoop stress over the wall must carry the projected inner contact force. Integrating the distribution gives \(\int_a^b(A+B/r^2)dr=A(b-a)+B(1/a-1/b)=60\) N/mm of axial depth. Dividing by wall thickness gives 3.00 MPa, precisely \(pa/(b-a)\). That average underpredicts the inner peak by 40%. Global balance is satisfied in both descriptions. A local comparison requires the radial distribution.

Keep the inside pressure fixed and increase the outer radius. Track both the inner hoop stress and the wall average. Before inspecting the thin-wall end of the slider, predict which pair of stress estimates will draw closest in relative terms.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · recover contact pressure from an outer strain

For the same ring, an outer circumferential gauge records \(50\) microstrain. Infer \(p\) and the inner hoop stress. What geometric assumption lets one gauge determine this field?

Invert the surface relation

At the free surface \(\sigma_\theta(b)=E\epsilon_\theta=3.50\) MPa. As \(\sigma_r(b)=0\), the hoop value is

\[2A=2pa^2/(b^2-a^2)=2p/3.\]

Thus \(p=5.25\) MPa and \(\sigma_\theta(a)=8.75\) MPa. Circumferentially uniform contact and known radii reduce the unknown traction to one number. A point load or an out-of-round sleeve would require more observations.

Further reading · A. F. Bower, Applied Mechanics of Solids, §4.1, axisymmetric elasticity

Contact pressure between elastic cylinders · Stress, deformation and structural measurement

Two polished cylindrical rollers touch along a narrow strip. Dividing load by a nominal full face area gives little indication of the actual pressure. Use the local shape of the gap and elastic half-space compliance to obtain the pressure distribution, and decide whether a measured contact width can determine material stiffness.

Chapter 16 · Creep, relaxation and what a holding test measures

Two polymer specimens can show the same initial force and quite different readings ten minutes later. Change the quantity held fixed and the same material reveals a different time scale.

Connect a spring to a dashpot and keep track of the variables

An ideal spring obeys \(\sigma=E\epsilon_s\). A linear dashpot obeys \(\sigma=\eta\dot\epsilon_d\), where the viscosity \(\eta\) has units Pa s. These elements describe recoverable storage and dissipative flow. Start with a Maxwell element by putting them in series. Both carry the same stress and their strains add, so differentiating total strain gives

\[\dot\epsilon=\frac{\dot\sigma}{E_1}+\frac{\sigma}{\eta}.\]

A fixed strain sets the left side to zero. Stored spring deformation slowly transfers to the dashpot while total extension stays fixed, producing a decaying stress. Under a constant nonzero stress the dashpot instead accumulates strain indefinitely. This second prediction makes the fluid-like long-time behaviour of the Maxwell element easy to test.

Add an equilibrium load path

Place a spring of modulus \(E_0\) parallel to the Maxwell element and call the Maxwell stress \(s\). Both branches now have the same total strain. The measured stress is \(E_0\epsilon+s\). Replace \(s\) by \(\sigma-E_0\epsilon\) in the series equation and gather the derivatives:

\[\dot\sigma+\frac{E_1}{\eta}\sigma=(E_0+E_1)\dot\epsilon+\frac{E_0E_1}{\eta}\epsilon .\]

In a sudden small strain step of magnitude \(\epsilon_*\), a finite dashpot cannot change strain instantaneously, so both springs respond at first. Holding the extension then allows the Maxwell stress to relax to zero. Conversely, a sudden stress step loads both springs at first but ends with the equilibrium spring carrying the complete applied stress. Solving the constant-coefficient equations with these initial and final conditions gives

\[\sigma(t)=\epsilon_*[E_0+E_1 e^{-t/\tau_r}],\]
\[\tau_r=\eta/E_1 ,\]
\[\epsilon(t)=\frac{\sigma_*}{E_0}+\left[\frac{\sigma_*}{E_0+E_1}-\frac{\sigma_*}{E_0}\right]e^{-t/\tau_c},\]
\[\tau_c=\frac{\eta(E_0+E_1)}{E_0E_1}.\]

The two exponential times differ because under load control the equilibrium spring gradually takes a different share of the force. At \(t=0\) the effective modulus is \(E_0+E_1\) in either test.

Worked example · identify parameters from one record

Impose 1% strain. Stress starts at 0.90 MPa and eventually settles at 0.30 MPa. The final value gives \(E_0=30\) MPa and the drop gives \(E_1=60\) MPa. Suppose the excess above the plateau falls to \(1/e\) of its initial value after 40 s. The viscosity is then \(60(40)=2400\) MPa s. After 80 s the stress prediction is

\[0.30+0.60e^{-2}=0.3812\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

Subtracting the plateau before fitting a logarithm is essential: the logarithm of the total stress has a changing slope.

Worked example · make a prediction the fit did not use

In an independent test impose a stress of 0.45 MPa. Strain jumps to \(0.45/90=0.005\) and approaches \(0.45/30=0.015\). The predicted creep time is

\[40(90/30)=120\,\mathrm{s}.\]

After one minute the prediction is

\[0.015-0.010e^{-0.5}=0.00893.\]

A model that used the 40 s relaxation time for creep would yield 0.01277, readily distinguishable if the load has settled. Both experiments should use the same temperature because molecular mobility, and therefore the viscosity, can be highly temperature dependent.

Double viscosity while leaving both spring moduli fixed. Predict which curve reaches its original one-minute value after two minutes. Next change the parallel spring alone and use the normalized curves to distinguish the two characteristic times.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · interrupt a relaxation experiment

Release the prescribed 1% strain instantaneously back to zero after 40 s. Calculate the stress immediately before and after release. What should happen over a further 40 s while zero extension is held?

Track the spring jumps

Before release the stress is

\[0.30+0.60/e=0.5207\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

At the abrupt negative strain step the dashpot strain cannot jump. Both springs together change stress by \(-0.01(90)=-0.90\) MPa, leaving −0.3793 MPa. At zero total strain the equilibrium spring carries zero and the remaining compressive Maxwell stress relaxes with the original 40 s time. It reaches −0.1395 MPa one relaxation time later.

Further reading · A. F. Bower, Applied Mechanics of Solids, §3.6, small-strain viscoelasticity

Chapter 17 · How a continuum balance becomes a finite-element stiffness matrix

A single bar element can predict an end displacement exactly and still miss the stress field inside. The difference follows directly from the balance it is asked to satisfy.

Turn a differential balance into work against a test displacement

Let a straight small-strain bar of length \(L\) have area \(A\), Young’s modulus \(E\), displacement \(u(x)\) and distributed axial load \(q(x)\) in N/m. Tensile axial resultant is \(N=EAu'\). Force balance on a slice gives \(N'+q=0\). Clamp the left end and prescribe a right-end force \(P\). Multiply the balance by any smooth virtual displacement \(v\) satisfying \(v(0)=0\), integrate along the bar, and integrate the derivative of \(N\) by parts.

\[\int_0^L EA\,u'v'\,dx=P\,v(L)+\int_0^L qv\,dx .\]

The boundary term at the clamp disappears because that displacement is prescribed. The unknown reaction can later be recovered by equilibrium. This formulation requires only first derivatives of trial displacements. Piecewise linear displacements are therefore admissible even though their slopes may jump at internal nodes.

Compute the stiffness rather than memorising it

On an element from \(x=0\) to \(h\), interpolate the two nodal displacements linearly:

\[u=(1-x/h)u_1+(x/h)u_2.\]

Let the test displacement have the same two basis functions. A virtual change of the first or second node gives the corresponding row of the balance. Both derivatives are constant with opposite signs.

\[B=\begin{bmatrix}-1/h&1/h\end{bmatrix},\qquad K_e=\int_0^h B^T EA B\,dx=\frac{EA}{h}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\-1&1\end{bmatrix}.\]

For a constant distributed load, each basis function integrates to \(h/2\), so each node receives equivalent load \(qh/2\). The two rows of stiffness sum to zero. A uniform translation stores no strain energy. At a shared node the neighbouring virtual-work contributions add because both elements refer to the same unknown displacement.

Worked example · compare a nodal value with the stress in a cell

Take a 1 m bar with \(EA=2.0\) MN, uniform \(q=1200\) N/m and free right end. Exact balance integrated from \(N(L)=0\) gives

\[N=q(L-x).\]

Integrating strain from the clamp produces \(u=q(Lx-x^2/2)/(EA)\). The free-end displacement is 0.300 mm. With a single element the free row of the discrete system has stiffness \(EA/L\) and load \(qL/2\), giving the same displacement. Yet its piecewise constant axial force is

\[EAu(L)/L=600\,\mathrm{N}.\]

The exact force ranges from 1200 N at the clamp to zero at the free end. The numerical force represents the mean over the cell.

Worked example · see what doubling resolution changes

Divide the bar into two equal elements. The middle node receives 600 N from both halves together, while the free node receives 300 N. The right element carries 300 N. Equilibrium of the middle node then puts 900 N in the left element. Their extensions are \(900(0.5)/(2\times10^6)=0.225\) mm and 0.075 mm. Nodal displacements agree with the exact quadratic at both nodes. The largest one-sided stress-resultant error within either element is now 300 N instead of 600 N. Comparing only tip displacements would hide this improvement.

Double the number of cells until the force error is below 100 N for the baseline load. Inspect the values on both sides of an element boundary and explain why agreement in tip displacement alone could have ended this refinement study too early.

Tutorial · find the support reaction from the discarded row

For the two-element calculation, recover the left reaction using the assembled loads and displacement. Then derive an expression for the maximum axial-force error with \(n\) equal elements. Why can two different mesh studies give two different conclusions?

Evaluate the clamp row and the local profile

The left internal nodal force is \(-900\) N. The left external distributed-load share is \(+300\) N. Equilibrium requires an additional reaction \(-1200\) N. On an element of width \(L/n\) the exact linearly varying force changes by \(qL/n\); its constant midpoint approximation misses an endpoint by \(qL/(2n)\). Tip displacement and maximum local force answer different engineering questions. Mesh convergence must therefore name its measured quantity.

Further reading · A. F. Bower, Applied Mechanics of Solids, §8.1, finite-element equations for elastic solids

Chapter 18 · Finding a displacement when no load acts in its direction

A displacement gauge measures the lateral travel of a truss joint under a vertical load. A force balance determines the bar forces, but cannot directly give the sideways motion. Introduce a dummy load, differentiate the stored energy and recover the gauge reading with its sign.

Link an external force coordinate to stored strain energy

For a linear elastic pin-jointed truss, neglect support movement and temperature changes and assume small displacements. Bar i has constant axial force \(N_i\), length \(L_i\), modulus \(E_i\) and area \(A_i\). Integrating its force against its extension gives

\[U_i=N_i^2L_i/(2E_iA_i).\]

Superposition makes each force linear in the external loads. A small increment of load changes the equilibrium configuration. For a linear elastic conservative system the complementary energy equals U and its derivative with respect to an independent applied force gives displacement along that force.

\[U=\sum_i{N_i^2L_i\over2E_iA_i},\]
\[\delta_Q={\partial U\over\partial Q}=\sum_i {N_i n_i L_i\over E_iA_i},\]
\[n_i={\partial N_i\over\partial Q}.\]

The derivative \(n_i\) is the member force produced per unit trial load in the requested direction, with all other independent loads held fixed. Solve this trial equilibrium even if \(Q=0\) physically. Setting \(Q=0\) before differentiating erases its entire contribution. As a dimensional check, \(n_i\) is dimensionless when Q and \(N_i\) both use newtons, and the sum has dimensions of length.

Compute the two-bar flexibility matrix

A joint hangs from two overhead pins by straight bars slanting up-left and up-right, each at 45° to horizontal. Positive joint coordinates are rightward and downward. Let V be the applied downward force and H the rightward force; bars carry tension positive. Equilibrium gives

\[N_L=(V+H)/\sqrt2,\ N_R=(V-H)/\sqrt2.\]

Define bar axial compliances \(c_i=L_i/(E_iA_i)\). Insert the forces into the energy and differentiate with respect to H and V independently:

\[\begin{bmatrix}\delta_H\\\delta_V\end{bmatrix}={1\over2}\begin{bmatrix}c_L+c_R&c_L-c_R\\c_L-c_R&c_L+c_R\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}H\\V\end{bmatrix}.\]

The cross coefficients are equal because the mixed derivatives of the stored energy commute. This is Maxwell–Betti reciprocity. A cross displacement can be negative even though both direct compliances and stored energy are positive. The eigenvalues of this particular flexibility matrix are \(c_L,c_R\), corresponding to the two independent bar-extension directions.

Predict a sideways gauge reading and reverse the test

Take \(c_L=0.0020,\ c_R=0.0040\) mm/N. At \(V=100\) N and \(H=0\), both members carry 70.71 N. The vertical displacement is \(0.300\) mm, and the horizontal displacement is −0.100 mm. The more compliant right bar stretches farther; the joint moves left and down to accommodate both bar extensions. Half the vertical load times its displacement gives 15 N mm of strain energy. Summing the two bar energies independently gives the same result.

Apply instead \(H=100\) N and \(V=0\), assuming the slender right bar remains stable under compression. The vertical reading is −0.100 mm and horizontal motion is +0.300 mm. Thus the lateral response to the vertical test predicts the vertical response to a lateral test of equal magnitude. Equalizing bar areas would remove the cross response if the lengths and moduli matched. In practice a pin with clearance or a cable that goes slack can break the assumed linear bilateral response.

Exchange the left and right compliances. Before looking at the new graph, mark which readout should reverse sign and which should remain equal. Then set the two values alike and identify the symmetry that removes cross-coupling.
